Boris Burkov

Patches Submitted (1)
Ongoing Submitted 2026-11-13 Last comment 2026-11-16 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH] btrfs: fix double release of data space on failed direct IO COW write Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch fixes a bug in the Btrfs file system where data space is released twice when a direct IO COW write fails, leading to an underflow of bytes_may_use and potential warnings or clamping to zero. The fix involves recording the extent consumed by btrfs_reserve_extent() in dio_data and releasing only the remaining reserved data space from bytes_may_use on error paths. This patch can be applied to stable kernels later than v5.19, which rewrote the affected error path.

Ongoing Submitted 2026-11-13 Last comment 2026-11-16 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH] mm/hmm: reject shadow-stack mappings Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch rejects shadow-stack mappings in the Heterogeneous Memory Management (HMM) module to prevent devices from gaining write access to user-space shadow stacks on x86 architectures.

reviewer noted that on x86, shadow-stack PTEs have their ordinary write bit clear but pte_write() reports them writable due to shadow-stack instructions; this allows HMM to grant devices write access to the process's shadow stack, potentially corrupting return addresses or bypassing protection
Show original comment 
Thanks.  This?

On x86, a shadow-stack PTE has its ordinary write bit clear.  The CPU
rejects ordinary stores, but pte_write() reports it writable because
shadow-stack instructions can write it.  HMM consequently sets
HMM_PFN_WRITE.  Nouveau uses that flag for GPU write permission, and
RDMA on-demand paging can use it to grant device write permission to a
registered memory region.  A device, or an RDMA peer with write access
to that region, can then overwrite the process's shadow stack,
potentially corrupting return addresses or bypassing that protection.

Reject shadow-stack VMAs in hmm_vma_walk_test(), including read access.
A requested fault returns -EFAULT; a snapshot records HMM_PFN_ERROR.

Signals: request for clarification, requested changes
Last comment 2026-11-17 [PATCH] mm/mempolicy: limit compat bitmap reads to the source nodemask Neutral LLM

This patch addresses a bug in the compat bitmap read for get_mempolicy() calls on 32-bit systems, where it can read past the kernel's stack nodemask due to an incorrect calculation of the number of bytes to copy. The fix bounds the compat bitmap read by MAX_NUMNODES and maintains existing behavior when the kernel nodemask is large enough.

3 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
David Hildenbrand (Arm) Reviewed-by Positive LLM
LGTM, but suggested reworking compat_put_bitmap() to handle bits instead of words for better alignment with copy_to_user().
Signals: LGTM
Arnd Bergmann ↳ replying to David Hildenbrand (Arm) Inline Review Needs Work LLM
Identified an off-by-one error in the calculation of copy bytes, which affects both native and compat syscalls. Suggested fixing this by aligning maxnode to 64 bits instead of 64 words.
Show original comment 
On Tue, Nov 17, 2026, at 11:19, Gregory Price (Meta) wrote:
> A 32-bit get_mempolicy() call can read past the kernel's stack nodemask.
> With 64 possible nodes and maxnode=65, copy_nodes_to_user() computes an
> eight-byte copy, but compat_put_bitmap() reads two native words.

I don't quite follow the explanation yet:

if maxnode=65, shouldn't that result in copying 16 bytes on x86-64
and 12 bytes on compat mode?

It looks like compat mode adjusts the wrong variable and we should
be doing this instead:

diff --git a/mm/mempolicy.c b/mm/mempolicy.c
index 6d662a9a8c74..f9a7be9f4432 100644
--- a/mm/mempolicy.c
+++ b/mm/mempolicy.c
@@ -1749,12 +1749,12 @@ static int get_nodes(nodemask_t *nodes, const unsigned long __user *nmask,
 static int copy_nodes_to_user(unsigned long __user *mask, unsigned long maxnode,
 			      nodemask_t *nodes)
 {
-	unsigned long copy = ALIGN(maxnode-1, 64) / 8;
+	unsigned long copy = ALIGN(maxnode-1, BITS_PER_LONG) / 8;
 	unsigned int nbytes = BITS_TO_LONGS(nr_node_ids) * sizeof(long);
 	bool compat = in_compat_syscall();
 
 	if (compat)
-		nbytes = BITS_TO_COMPAT_LONGS(nr_node_ids) * sizeof(compat_long_t);
+		copy = ALIGN(maxnode-1, BITS_PER_COMPAT_LONG) / 8;
 
 	if (copy > nbytes) {
 		if (copy > PAGE_SIZE)

Now in the maxnode=65 example, it should clear 4 bytes and put copy
the 8 byte bitmap.

      Arnd
Signals: off-by-one error
Arnd Bergmann Needs Work LLM
Raised concerns about the ABI implications of changing the behavior for 32-bit userspace asking for maxnum that results in an odd number of 32-bit words.
Show original comment 
On Tue, Nov 17, 2026, at 13:44, Arnd Bergmann wrote:
> On Tue, Nov 17, 2026, at 11:19, Gregory Price (Meta) wrote:
>> A 32-bit get_mempolicy() call can read past the kernel's stack nodemask.
>> With 64 possible nodes and maxnode=65, copy_nodes_to_user() computes an
>> eight-byte copy, but compat_put_bitmap() reads two native words.
>
> I don't quite follow the explanation yet:
>
> if maxnode=65, shouldn't that result in copying 16 bytes on x86-64
> and 12 bytes on compat mode?
>
> It looks like compat mode adjusts the wrong variable and we should
> be doing this instead:
>
> diff --git a/mm/mempolicy.c b/mm/mempolicy.c
> index 6d662a9a8c74..f9a7be9f4432 100644
> --- a/mm/mempolicy.c
> +++ b/mm/mempolicy.c
> @@ -1749,12 +1749,12 @@ static int get_nodes(nodemask_t *nodes, const 
> unsigned long __user *nmask,
>  static int copy_nodes_to_user(unsigned long __user *mask, unsigned 
> long maxnode,
>  			      nodemask_t *nodes)
>  {
> -	unsigned long copy = ALIGN(maxnode-1, 64) / 8;
> +	unsigned long copy = ALIGN(maxnode-1, BITS_PER_LONG) / 8;

I guess this would be an ABI change for 32-bit userspace asking for
maxnum that results in an odd number of 32-bit words. The man
page documents this as "The value specified by maxnode is always
rounded to a multiple of sizeof(unsigned long)*8", while the
code here has always rounded it up to a multiple of 64 in
both native (no longer supported on 32-bit) and compat syscalls.

numactl (1) always uses 128 or 2048 bit masks and won't notice
it either way, so we can possibly change the ABI here and
do what the man page says, or keep the current behavior and update
the man page.

It looks like the other part of the problem is the off-by-one
error: the "ALIGN(maxnode-1, 64) / 8" rounds down maxnode=65
to copy=8 which is the same as nbytes, but the compat_put_bitmap()
treats it as a 65-bit map that needs 96 bytes. 

This means we have a related bug on native syscalls as well:
asking for a 65 bit mask in mempolicy only returns 8 bytes
without clearing the next word like it would for a 66 bit mask.
If we just fix the off-by-one and keep the 64 bit alignment
(with a manpage update), the version below be should be sufficient.

   Arnd

--- a/mm/mempolicy.c
+++ b/mm/mempolicy.c
@@ -1749,13 +1749,10 @@ static int get_nodes(nodemask_t *nodes, const unsigned long __user *nmask,
 static int copy_nodes_to_user(unsigned long __user *mask, unsigned long maxnode,
                              nodemask_t *nodes)
 {
-       unsigned long copy = ALIGN(maxnode-1, 64) / 8;
+       unsigned long copy = ALIGN(maxnode, 64) / 8;
        unsigned int nbytes = BITS_TO_LONGS(nr_node_ids) * sizeof(long);
        bool compat = in_compat_syscall();
 
-       if (compat)
-               nbytes = BITS_TO_COMPAT_LONGS(nr_node_ids) * sizeof(compat_long_t);
-
        if (copy > nbytes) {
                if (copy > PAGE_SIZE)
                        return -EINVAL;

Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch introduces the concept of 'common memory' as a separate entity from NUMA nodes that have memory, allowing for private node memory to be isolated from the main memory management system. The patch creates a new flag N_MEMORY_COMMON and converts existing users of N_MEMORY to use this new flag instead. This is the first phase of a series that aims to introduce private NUMA nodes and allow userland to manage them via mbind() and migrate_pages().

21 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Gregory (Meta) (author) Positive LLM
The author addressed a concern about the lack of explicit node eligibility filtering in MM walkers by adding helper functions/macros to select node eligibility explicitly, and iterators for nodemask intersection and state iteration without allocating temporary nodemasks.
Show original comment 
Add node-state-filter helper functions/macros so MM walkers can select
their node eligibility explicitly.

Add nodemask intersection and nodemask/state iterators using the bitmap
intersection helpers, without allocating a temporary nodemask.

Signed-off-by: Gregory Price (Meta) <gourry@gourry.net>

 include/linux/nodemask.h | 28 ++++++++++++++++++++----

diff --git a/include/linux/mmzone.h b/include/linux/mmzone.h
index c161d563ce8fd..5ff190d870830 100644
--- a/include/linux/mmzone.h
+++ b/include/linux/mmzone.h
@@ -1845,6 +1845,13 @@ extern struct zone *next_zone(struct zone *zone);
 			; /* do nothing */		\
 		else
 
+/* Populated zones on nodes in @state. */
+#define for_each_populated_zone_state(zone, state)	\
+	for_each_populated_zone(zone)			\
+		if (!node_state(zone_to_nid(zone), state))	\
+			; /* do nothing */		\
+		else
+
 static inline struct zone *zonelist_zone(struct zoneref *zoneref)
 {
 	return zoneref->zone;
@@ -1937,6 +1944,45 @@ static inline struct zoneref *first_zones_zonelist(struct zonelist *zonelist,
 			zone = zonelist_zone(z))
 
 
+static inline struct zoneref *
+next_zones_zonelist_state(struct zoneref *z, enum zone_type highest_zoneidx,
+			  const nodemask_t *nodes, enum node_states state)
+{
+	z = next_zones_zonelist(z, highest_zoneidx, nodes);
+	while (zonelist_zone(z) && !node_state(zonelist_node_idx(z), state))
+		z = next_zones_zonelist(++z, highest_zoneidx, nodes);
+	return z;
+}
+
+static inline struct zoneref *
+first_zones_zonelist_state(struct zonelist *zonelist,
+			   enum zone_type highest_zoneidx,
+			   const nodemask_t *nodes, enum node_states state)
+{
+	return next_zones_zonelist_state(zonelist->_zonerefs, highest_zoneidx,
+					 nodes, state);
+}
+
+/**
+ * for_each_zone_node_state - iterate zones in intersect(nodemask, node_state(state))
+ * @zone: The current zone in the iterator
+ * @z: The current pointer within zonelist->_zonerefs being iterated
+ * @zlist: The zonelist being iterated
+ * @highidx: The zone index of the highest zone to return
+ * @nodemask: Nodemask allowed by the allocator
+ * @state: The node state the zone's node must be in
+ *
+ * The state filter is part of the iteration rather than a trailing if, so that
+ * this expands to a single for statement: a macro ending in an unbraced if
+ * silently steals a following else from the caller.
+ */
+#define for_each_zone_node_state(zone, z, zlist, highidx, nodemask, state)	\
+	for (z = first_zones_zonelist_state(zlist, highidx, nodemask, state),	\

2.53.1-Meta
View on lore ↗
Signals: acknowledged a fix is needed, centralized updates
Gregory (Meta) (author) Neutral LLM
The author addressed a concern about the overloaded meaning of N_MEMORY by clarifying that it should only mean a node has memory, and introducing N_MEMORY_COMMON to mean a node's memory is reachable via ordinary allocation or MM service. The author explained that every node with memory is common, so N_MEMORY_COMMON equals N_MEMORY and there is no behavioral change.
Show original comment 
N_MEMORY is overloaded with two meanings:
  1) a node has memory.
  2) a node's memory is reachable via ordinary allocation or
     an arbitrary MM service may use that memory.

Allow N_MEMORY to mean #1, and introduce N_MEMORY_COMMON to mean #2.

N_MEMORY_COMMON nodes contain "common memory" capable of being managed
arbitrarily by the kernel MM based solely on zone membership. Following
commits convert relevant N_MEMORY users to N_MEMORY_COMMON.

Add folio_is_common_memory() and page_is_common_memory() for MM services
that walk folios. Following commits convert folio_is_zone_device() and
page_is_zone_device() callers accordingly.

Expose the state via `has_common_memory` so userspace can identify
the memory available to ordinary allocations and common MM services.

Add has_memory to the docs while we're here.

Every node with memory is common, so N_MEMORY_COMMON equals N_MEMORY
and there is no behavioral change.

Signed-off-by: Gregory Price (Meta) <gourry@gourry.net>

2.53.1-Meta
View on lore ↗
Signals: clarification, explanation
Gregory (Meta) (author) Positive LLM
The author addressed a concern about the zonelists deciding which nodes an allocation can reach, explaining that build_local_zonelist() refactor allows reusing code to build local-only zonelists for nodes outside N_MEMORY_COMMON set. The author confirmed that every node with memory is common and that the zonelists are unchanged.
Show original comment 
Zonelists decide which nodes an allocation can reach.

The fallback lists hold every node in N_MEMORY, and a node's
__GFP_THISNODE list holds its own zones whatever the node's state.

Ordinary allocations should reach only common (general purpose) memory.

Build the fallback zonelists from N_MEMORY_COMMON, and __GFP_THISNODE
zonelists only for a common node.

The build_local_zonelist() refactor lets use reuse the code to build
a local-only zonelist for nodes outside the N_MEMORY_COMMON set later.

Every node with memory is common, and a memoryless node has no zones to
list, so the zonelists are unchanged.

Signed-off-by: Gregory Price (Meta) <gourry@gourry.net>

Gregory (Meta) (author) Needs Work LLM
The author addressed a concern about the boot mempolicy calculations and their per-node sysfs entries, which were using N_MEMORY instead of N_MEMORY_COMMON. The author agreed to change this in the patch by restricting the node set mpol_set_nodemask() applies to set_mempolicy() and mbind() masks to N_MEMORY_COMMON nodes.
Show original comment 
Use N_MEMORY_COMMON for boot mempolicy calculations and their per-node
sysfs entries (weighted interleave weights). 

Restrict the node set mpol_set_nodemask() applies to set_mempolicy()
and mbind() masks to N_MEMORY_COMMON nodes.

Later when an N_MEMORY node is omitted frmo the N_MEMORY_COMMON set,
they will be stripped from mempolicy nodemasks as they're not eligible
(similar to memoryless nodes today).

All memory is common at this point, no behavior change at this point.

The author is addressing a concern about the single-node optimization in hashdist, explaining that it should count N_MEMORY_COMMON nodes instead of N_MEMORY nodes. They confirm that all memory is common as of now and behavior remains unchanged.
Show original comment

multiple NUMA nodes. The tables come from ordinary allocations, which
only N_MEMORY_COMMON nodes serve.

Count N_MEMORY_COMMON nodes when applying the single-node optimization.

All memory is common as of now, so behavior is unchanged.

2.53.1-Meta
View on lore ↗
Signals: clarification, explanation
Gregory (Meta) (author) Needs Work LLM
The author addressed a concern about migration targets being set to N_MEMORY instead of N_MEMORY_COMMON, which can lead to unusable destination nodes during partial offlining. The author agreed that the hot-unplug migration target-list should be restricted to N_MEMORY_COMMON and provided a patch with the necessary changes.
Show original comment 
do_migrate_range() / alloc_migration_target() is only capable of
allocating memory on N_MEMORY_COMMON nodes. The destination nodemask
presently starts with N_MEMORY and removes the source-node.

In the current code, if the destination mask becomes empty, the source
node is restored to allow migration during partial offlining.

Consider this configuration:
  Node0 = N_MEMORY_COMMON
  Node1 = N_MEMORY, not N_MEMORY_COMMON

Node 1 keeps the destination mask non-empty, despite being unreachable.
This leaves migration with no usable destination because the source
node is not an eligible allocation target.

Restrict the hot-unplug migration target-list to N_MEMORY_COMMON so it
reflects the nodes the allocator can reach.

All memory is common at this point - behavior is unchanged.

Signed-off-by: Gregory Price (Meta) <gourry@gourry.net>

2.53.1-Meta

Gregory (Meta) (author) Neutral LLM
The author addressed a concern about the memory demotion paths and default DRAM performance reference being based on every node that has memory, rather than just N_MEMORY_COMMON. The author explained that this is already the case in the current implementation, where all memory nodes carry N_MEMORY_COMMON, so behavior remains unchanged.
Show original comment 
Memory outside N_MEMORY_COMMON is outside the ordinary allocation pool
and cannot be a generic demotion source or destination.

Build demotion paths and the default DRAM performance reference from
N_MEMORY_COMMON rather than every node that has memory, and do not
assign other nodes to a memory tier through the generic hotplug path.

Removing a node's memory already resets its demotion targets, so a node
that unplugs and returns outside N_MEMORY_COMMON keeps none.

All memory nodes currently carry N_MEMORY_COMMON, behavior is unchanged.

Signed-off-by: Gregory Price (Meta) <gourry@gourry.net>

Signals: acknowledged, agreed
Richard Cheng ↳ replying to Gregory (Meta) Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Richard Cheng noted that there are two mechanisms to enforce the 'common memory only' rule: zonelists built from N_MEMORY_COMMON and checks at call sites using node_state() or folio_is_common_memory(). However, he found inconsistencies where some paths use both mechanisms, while others use only one. He requested clarification on which mechanism is responsible for each path, particularly when a non-common node gets its own zonelist.
Show original comment 
Hi Gregory,

I've browsed through the serie, I'll go into detail in each patch
in the following days, for now I have one general question.

I wonder how the "common memory only" rule is enforced ?

As far as I read it, there're 2 ways:
1. The zonelist, you build them from N_MEMORY_COMMON, so non-common
   node isn't in any zonelist.
2. A check at the call site, e.g. node_state(nid, N_MEMORY_COMMON) or
   folio_is_common_memory()


Code reaches a node's memory in 3 ways: zonelist, node id, or by folio.
I couldn't find a statement of which mechanism is responsible for which
kind of path.

I see some consistent issues,
1. zonelist paths are covered by way number 1. Some of them also carry
   way number 2, some don't
2. node id and folio paths can only be covered by way number 2. Some entry
   points have the check, and some do not.

I can't tell when a call site has no check is deliberate or missed.
This matters once non-common node gets its own zonelist, as patch 4 metioned I think?

Could you clarify the rule ? e.g. which zonelist users must filter on N_MEMORY_COMMON
themselves? and why the zonelist alone is not enough for them ?
Whether every path that takes a node id or a folio must check N_MEMORY_COMMON, and
which ones are left for the feature bits in phase 3 ?

Let's get the rule settled down and make it as the foundation as well as phase 2 and
phase 3 ?

Best regards,
Richard Cheng.
View on lore ↗
Signals: inconsistency, unclear
Richard Cheng Inline Review Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Richard Cheng noted that the new code may lead to uneven or smaller-than-requested huge page pools due to delayed node addition, and suggested placing nodes_and() inside the loop to restore the original behavior.
Show original comment 
For a global request the caller passes &node_states[N_MEMORY], so before this patch
the grow loop read the live mask on every alloc_pool_huge_folio().
Now the mask is sampled once, before the loop.

The loop drops hugetlb_lock and can run for a long time. If a node gets its first
memoroy in that window, the old code would start using it. The new code won't, until
the next write to nr_hugepages.

So the pool can end up uneven, or smaller than requested if the other nodes are full.

I think the nodes_and() should be placed inside the loop to keep the old behavior ?

Best regards,
Richard Cheng.

memory. THis now does up to 3 set_bit() calls on node_states[] for every onlined block.
The result is the same, but node_states[] is __read_mostly. A test-before-set in the helper
would keep the old behavior, or if you would like to keep it, please address in commit message.

Richard Cheng.

Signals: questioning consistency, requesting clarification
Gregory (Meta) (author) ↳ replying to Richard Cheng Needs Work LLM
The author acknowledged that the existing code already handles the two cases mentioned by Richard Cheng and explained that the series largely modifies these locations to use N_MEMORY_COMMON instead. The author is addressing concerns about how to reach a node's folios/pages, specifically in the context of page allocator freelists and pfn walks. They provided three categories of methods: kernel structures (page tables, VMAs, etc.), pfn walks (memory hot-unplug, contiguous allocation, DAMON), and the page allocator itself (freelists). The author acknowledged that some filtering is already done by existing code and agreed to add additional filtering in a later patch to reduce complexity. The author is addressing concerns about premature changes in the patch series. They acknowledge that some code should be moved to later phases (N_MEMORY_PRIVATE and N_MEMORY_FEATURE) and will restructure the current phase to handle cases for walking zonelists, nodelists, folios, and private zonelists in order.
Show original comment 
Those are essentially the two cases, which are already present in the
code in one shape or another in the existing shape:

   node_state(nid, N_MEMORY);
        or
   folio_is_zone_device(folio)

With the exception of one or two places, the series largely modifies
these locations to use N_MEMORY_COMMON instead (or in addition, in the
case of zone device).  There are a few places we add common checks where
zone device checks were not already present (because they were missing
or did not apply, usually PMD scenarios).

---

Being a bit more precise: How do we reach a node's folios/pages?

 1) through some kernel structure, obvious examples:
    - page tables
    - VMAs / pagemaps / xarrays
    - LRUs

 2) pfn walks
    - memory hot-unplug
    - contiguous allocation
    - DAMON

 3) page allocator
    - freelists (page allocator)
    Sort of its own category since you ask it for folios from a node

---

Some of this is obfuscated by the fact that the existing code already
does this filtering for you.

I suppose I have some premature changes to add additional filtering
where it isn't needed yet to reduce the complexity of later patches,
but I can see why this is confusing when introduced by itself.

Consider this:

-       for_each_zone_zonelist_nodemask(zone, z, zonelist,
-                                       gfp_zone(gfp_mask), nodemask) {
+       for_each_zone_node_state(zone, z, zonelist, gfp_zone(gfp_mask),
+                                nodemask, N_MEMORY_COMMON) {

This is a bit of a preparatory change to later convert this to:

+       for_each_zone_node_state(zone, z, zonelist, gfp_zone(gfp_mask),
+                                nodemask, N_MEMORY_RECLAIM) {

It looks like we're filtering by N_MEMORY_COMMON, but we're actually
iterating the fallback zonelist (constructed from N_MEMORY_COMMON)

  struct zonelist *zonelist = node_zonelist(numa_node_id(), sc.gfp_mask);

Later we update this node_zonelist() call to determine whether the
private nodelist should be walked, at which point you do have to filter
for service support.

---

So:
  1) If you're walking the normal zonelists, you're already filtered

  2) If you're walking nodelists, you need to filter on N_MEMORY_COMMON

  3) If you're operating on folios, you need to check common_memory()

  4) If you're walking the private zonelist, filter on N_MEMORY_COMMON


So let me do this:
  1) i'll restructure this phase to omit premature changes like above
     (they'll move into N_MEMORY_PRIVATE and N_MEMORY_<FEATURE> commits).

  2) I'll restructure N_MEMORY_COMMON commits here to handle cases 1-3
     above in that order, and that'll hopefully clear up the confusion.

-- 
~Gregory

Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch introduces a new set of functions to iterate over nodes based on their state, such as online or memory state. It adds iterators for walking through node states and provides helpers for intersecting nodemasks with specific node states. This allows developers to easily traverse the system's nodes based on their current state, making it easier to manage and optimize system resources.

11 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Gregory (Meta) Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer noted that the patch series does not provide a clear explanation of how private nodes interact with existing kernel services, and requested more information on the node state use/filtering model.
Show original comment 
This is v6 of the Private NUMA node series. The series falls into four
parts, each building on the one before:

   1-18  mm: introduce N_MEMORY_COMMON
  19-26  restrict folio-walking MM services to common memory
  27-44  N_MEMORY_PRIVATE, kept out of cpusets, mempolicy, accounting
         and the page allocator's ordinary paths
  45-61  private node features, memory hotplug of private memory,
         dax/kmem private mode, selftests and documentation

Patches 1-26 change no behavior on any system and can be reviewed and
merged on their own. Without private nodes, every path in patches 27-61
behaves as before.

The full stack is also available at:
https://github.com/gourryinverse/linux/tree/scratch/gourry/managed_nodes/private_nodes_v6

Motivation
==========

Some memory should be visible as a NUMA node without becoming part of
the general allocation pool: HBM, coherent GPU or accelerator memory,
or device memory a driver hands out itself.

Today such memory is either ordinary System RAM, where any allocation
can fall back onto it and every MM service manages it, or ZONE_DEVICE,
where the buddy allocator and the MM services are not available at all.

Private nodes sit in between. Their memory is managed by the buddy
allocator, but ordinary allocations never land on it, and generic MM
services leave it alone unless the node opts back in.

Model
=====

  N_MEMORY = N_MEMORY_COMMON + N_MEMORY_PRIVATE

  - N_MEMORY keeps meaning "this node has memory".
  - N_MEMORY_COMMON means ordinary allocations and kernel services
    may use it.
  - Allocation isolation comes from the zonelists: a private node is
    on no fallback zonelist and has an empty __GFP_THISNODE zonelist.
    It is reached only through its own private zonelist.
  - Services that walk existing folios check folio_is_common_memory()
    where they already skip ZONE_DEVICE.
  - A private node can opt into compaction, reclaim and userspace
    placement through per-node features. The services are written in
    terms of their own node state (N_MEMORY_RECLAIM, ...), so services
    don't need to be "aware" of anything but themselves.

The node state use / filtering model goes as such:
  1) If you're walking the normal zonelists, you're already filtered
  2) If you're walking nodelists to touch memory, filter on
     N_MEMORY_COMMON by default (replacing N_MEMORY).
  3) If you're walking structures to operate on folios / pages,
     check folio_is_common_memory() (replacing zone_device checks)
  4) If the service allows private memory interactions, use the
     N_MEMORY_<FEATURE> bit corresponding to the service.

     e.g.: reclaim checks N_MEMORY_FEATURE when walking N_MEMORY
     or when walking the private memory zonelist.

N_MEMORY_COMMON (patches 1-18)
==============================

N_MEMORY has two meanings today:
  1) this node has memory
  2) ordinary allocations and MM services can use this node's memory

The two are the same today. They stop being the same once a NUMA node
with memory isn't part of the general allocation pool.

N_MEMORY_COMMON takes the second meaning, and the N_MEMORY users that
mean it are converted. N_MEMORY continues reporting memory topology.

The rule for choosing between them:

  N_MEMORY         the node has memory: topology, per-node metadata and
                   statistics, cpuset.mems.
  N_MEMORY_COMMON  ordinary allocations or a kernel service may use the
                   node's memory: zonelists, allocation and migration
                   targets, per-node service threads, and sizing or
                   accounting meant for ordinary allocations.

Code that walks the existing zonelist needs no check. The zonelists
are built from N_MEMORY_COMMON, so they already hold only memory the
allocation may use.

Unless a system actually has a private node, N_MEMORY and
N_MEMORY_COMMON are the same set, so behavior should be unchanged.

  1-3   node-state helpers, N_MEMORY_COMMON itself, and the
        `has_common_memory` sysfs ABI.
  4     zonelists: the fallback lists hold common nodes, and a node's
        __GFP_THISNODE list is built only for a common node
  5-11  allocation and migration targets
  12-13 kswapd and kcompactd
  14-18 sizing and accounting meant for ordinary allocations

has_common_memory lists the same nodes as has_memory until memory
outside N_MEMORY_COMMON exists. It is added now so that userspace, such
as libnuma, can start choosing allocation targets from it, and so that
the meaning is fixed before anything makes the two lists differ.

Folio-walking MM services (patches 19-26)
=========================================

Patches 1-18 decide where memory comes from: zonelists, allocation
targets and per-node services follow N_MEMORY_COMMON. Some MM services
instead reach folios that already exist, wherever they are: through
page tables (KSM, khugepaged, NUMA hinting, madvise, mbind() and
migrate_pages(), move_pages()) or through physical addresses (DAMON).

These services skip ZONE_DEVICE folios with folio_is_zone_device() and
page_is_zone_device().

Patch 19 generalizes those checks into folio_is_common_memory() and
page_is_common_memory(), which reject ZONE_DEVICE folios and folios
on a node outside N_MEMORY_COMMON.

The rule: a service that walks folios checks folio_is_common_memory()
where it would otherwise check folio_is_zone_device().

Rather than add new hooks to page-table and pfn walkers, this reuses the
places that already opt ZONE_DEVICE memory out.

  19    folio_is_common_memory() and page_is_common_memory()
  20-26 ZONE_DEVICE checks in folio-walking services become
        page/folio_is_common_memory(). One component per patch.

N_MEMORY_PRIVATE (patches 27-44)
================================

Patches 27-44 add the memory that makes N_MEMORY and N_MEMORY_COMMON
differ. A private node has online System RAM that ordinary allocations
do not use and MM services leave alone, so neither an allocation that
names no node nor a node-local kernel allocation can land on it.

Most of these patches add the explicit opt-outs that the N_MEMORY ->
N_MEMORY_COMMON conversion did not cover, largely concentrated in
cpuset, mempolicy and migration.

In the page allocator, each node gets a private zonelist holding its
own zones only if it is private. MM code reaches private memory only
through that zonelist (ALLOC_ZONELIST_PRIVATE plus a nodemask).

A private request whose node becomes ineligible relaxes onto common
memory, and exhausting private memory fails the allocation (SIGBUS on
fault) instead of invoking the OOM killer (this can change in the
future with additional work).

Nothing in patches 27-44 creates private memory. Memory hotplug of
private memory, with dax/kmem as its first user, comes in patches
56-57, after the feature bits that let a private node opt into
reclaim, compaction and userspace placement.

  27-28 N_MEMORY_PRIVATE, exported as has_private_memory.
        A per-node feature mask on the pgdat.
  29-30 accounting: MemTotal/MemFree/other limits ignore private mem.
        the auto-movable online policy ignores private memory
  31-35 cpusets and mempolicy: private nodes are inherited only on
        request, never rebound onto, never spread over, and never
        migrate_pages() destinations
  36    explicit reclaim and compaction requests refuse private nodes
  37-44 page allocator:
        37-38 the reserves and watermarks, and contiguous allocation,
              skip/refuse private memory
        39-40 the private zonelist and its selection by MM callers
        41    a private request whose node becomes ineligible relaxes
              onto common memory to ensure forward progress
        42    exhausting private memory fails with SIGBUS, like an
              exhausted HugeTLB pool
        43    an alloc_flags-aware folio allocator (Brendan Jackman)
        44    a bind policy whose nodes became private falls back to
              common memory

Private node features and providers (patches 45-61)
===================================================

Patches 1-44 keep every generic MM service off private memory.

Some private memory wants a few of them back: HBM or coherent GPU
memory that userspace manages with mbind() and migrate_pages() needs
reclaim and compaction, while staying out of incidental allocation
(no fallback onto it, only explicit placement).

Patches 45-55 add three opt-in feature bits and corresponding node
states:

  N_MEMORY_COMPACTION   kcompactd, proactive and explicit compaction
  N_MEMORY_RECLAIM      kswapd, memcg and explicit reclaim
  N_MEMORY_MEMPOLICY    mbind() and explicit migration may target it

The states cover what the kernel does on its own initiative. Direct
reclaim and compaction serve an allocation within its own zonelist, so
an allocation from a private node may reclaim and compact it either way.

RECLAIM requires COMPACTION, and MEMPOLICY requires both. Userspace
control (mbind, migration) generates fragmentation, and as a result
private allocations would fail unpredictably without reclaim/compaction.

Relcaim and compaction are required by mempolicy because otherwise
order-N allocations cause unpredictable out of memory conditions due
to fragmentation.

Demotion and NUMA balancing support are deferred to a later series
but can be trivially support to allow demotions from HBM to DRAM
as a way of cheap tiering.

Every common node carries all three, so the existing N_MEMORY_COMMON
users that mean "reclaim" or "compaction" move to the new states.

Service support:

  45-46 N_MEMORY_COMPACTION, and compaction switched to it
  47-48 N_MEMORY_RECLAIM, and reclaim switched to it
  49    madvise reclaim advice only for reclaim-capable memory
  50    memcg reclaim also visits private nodes that permit reclaim
  51    N_MEMORY_MEMPOLICY
  52    explicit migration acts on N_MEMORY_MEMPOLICY memory
  53    N_MEMORY_MEMPOLICY memory counts as general-purpose.
        MemAvailable (free and file pages) and dirty limits count
	only common memory.
  54    MPOL_F_PRIVATE_NODES: mbind() flag that binds a mapping to
        one private node.
  55    migrate_pages() support for N_MEMORY_MEMPOLICY nodes

Creating a private node

  56    memory hotplug of private memory, with a feature mask
  57    dax/kmem private mode

Testing and Documentation:

  58-60 selftests: private kmem devices and services, memory policy
        bindings, migration and exhaustion, and cpuset interaction
  61    Documentation/mm/numa_private_nodes.rst

MPOL_F_PRIVATE_NODES is new uapi. Without it, private nodes in an
mbind() mask are ignored, similar to nodes outside the cpuset.

libnuma and numactl need a matching change to reach private nodes.

There is no sysfs list of the nodes that accept private bindings.
userspace can probe with MPOL_F_PRIVATE_NODES.  Otherwise - we can
discuss how service support should be discoverable to userspace.

Memory hotplug is the only source of private memory right now.

All System RAM on a node shares one configuration: hotplug with a
different one fails with -EBUSY.

Costs and known limits
======================

  - ZONELIST_PRIVATE is a third zonelist per pgdat; with NODES_SHIFT=10
    that is about 80KB per possible node, though a private zonelist
    holds at most one node's zones.

    local-only zonelists can be space-optimized if this matters.

  - Free page reporting reports free private pages like any others.

  - Tasks are OOM-scored by all of their resident memory,
    private pages included.

Changes since v5 ("Private Memory NUMA Nodes")
==============================================

  - Reordered so the N_MEMORY audit (patches 1-26) comes first and can
    be reviewed (and merged) on its own if desired.
  - Capabilities became memory features with node states, so a service
    can be written in terms of itself: reclaim walks N_MEMORY_RECLAIM,
    compaction N_MEMORY_COMPACTION.
  - The capability set is pared down to COMPACTION, RECLAIM and
    MEMPOLICY for the first run.  DEMOTION, NUMA_BALANCING, and
    compressed RAM support are deferred.
  - The private zonelist is now local-only. A fallback variant can be
    added if a use case needs one.
  - cpuset and OOM semantics are worked out:
      - cpuset.mems governs private nodes
      - private-only request inherits the parent's common nodes.
  - Private bindings are an explicit uapi flag, MPOL_F_PRIVATE_NODES,
    rather than inferred from the nodemask.
  - Exhausting private placement fails the fault with SIGBUS, as an
    exhausted HugeTLB pool does, instead of invoking the OOM killer.
    OOM support can be added later, but this keeps initial support
    reasonable simple.

Signed-off-by: Gregory Price (Meta) <gourry@gourry.net>

2.56.1
View on lore ↗
Signals: requested changes
Gregory Relay (author) ↳ replying to Gregory (Meta) Needs Work LLM
The author addressed a concern about the overloaded meaning of N_MEMORY, which is used to indicate both nodes with memory and nodes whose memory can be managed by kernel MM services. The author introduced a new state N_MEMORY_COMMON to mean #2, and modified documentation and code accordingly.
Show original comment 
From: "Gregory Price (Meta)" <gourry@gourry.net>

N_MEMORY is overloaded with two meanings:
  1) a node has memory.
  2) a node's memory is reachable via ordinary allocation or
     an arbitrary MM service may use that memory.

Allow N_MEMORY to mean #1, and introduce N_MEMORY_COMMON to mean #2.

N_MEMORY_COMMON nodes contain "common memory" capable of being managed
arbitrarily by the kernel MM based solely on zone membership. Following
commits convert relevant N_MEMORY users to N_MEMORY_COMMON.

Expose the state via `has_common_memory` so userspace can identify
the memory available to ordinary allocations and common MM services.

Add has_memory to the docs while we're here.

Signed-off-by: Gregory Price (Meta) <gourry@gourry.net>
---

2.56.1
View on lore ↗
Signals: acknowledged a fix is needed
Gregory Relay (author) ↳ replying to Gregory (Meta) Neutral LLM
The author addressed a concern about the build_zonelists() function building fallback zonelists from N_MEMORY instead of N_MEMORY_COMMON, and explained that this is intentional because ordinary allocations should reach only common (general purpose) memory. The author also mentioned that the build_local_zonelist() refactor allows for reusing code to build a local-only zonelist for nodes outside the N_MEMORY_COMMON set later.
Show original comment 
From: "Gregory Price (Meta)" <gourry@gourry.net>

Zonelists decide which nodes an allocation can reach.

The fallback lists hold every node in N_MEMORY, and a node's
__GFP_THISNODE list holds its own zones whatever the node's state.

Ordinary allocations should reach only common (general purpose) memory.

Build the fallback zonelists from N_MEMORY_COMMON, and __GFP_THISNODE
zonelists only for a common node.

The build_local_zonelist() refactor lets us reuse the code to build
a local-only zonelist for nodes outside the N_MEMORY_COMMON set later.

Signed-off-by: Gregory Price (Meta) <gourry@gourry.net>
---

View on lore ↗
Signals: clarification, explanation
Gregory Relay (author) ↳ replying to Gregory (Meta) Needs Work LLM
The author addressed a concern about the boot mempolicy calculations and their per-node sysfs entries, which should only be applied to N_MEMORY_COMMON nodes. The author agreed to make this change by restricting the node set mpol_set_nodemask() applies to set_mempolicy() and mbind() masks to N_MEMORY_COMMON nodes.
Show original comment 
From: "Gregory Price (Meta)" <gourry@gourry.net>

Use N_MEMORY_COMMON for boot mempolicy calculations and their per-node
sysfs entries (weighted interleave weights).

Restrict the node set mpol_set_nodemask() applies to set_mempolicy()
and mbind() masks to N_MEMORY_COMMON nodes.

A tmpfs mpol= nodelist must name only N_MEMORY_COMMON nodes, and an
interleave policy without a nodelist defaults to them.

Later when an N_MEMORY node is omitted from the N_MEMORY_COMMON set,
they will be stripped from mempolicy nodemasks as they're not eligible
(similar to memoryless nodes today).

Signed-off-by: Gregory Price (Meta) <gourry@gourry.net>

2.56.1
View on lore ↗
Signals: missing-implementation, undefined-behavior
Gregory Relay (author) ↳ replying to Gregory (Meta) Needs Work LLM
The author is addressing a concern that the hot-unplug migration target-list should only include N_MEMORY_COMMON nodes, which are reachable by the allocator. The author agrees and proposes to restrict the destination nodemask in do_migrate_range() to node_states[N_MEMORY_COMMON].
Show original comment 
From: "Gregory Price (Meta)" <gourry@gourry.net>

do_migrate_range() / alloc_migration_target() is only capable of
allocating memory on N_MEMORY_COMMON nodes. The destination nodemask
presently starts with N_MEMORY and removes the source-node.

In the current code, if the destination mask becomes empty, the source
node is restored to allow migration during partial offlining.

Consider this configuration:
  Node0 = N_MEMORY_COMMON
  Node1 = N_MEMORY, not N_MEMORY_COMMON

Node 1 keeps the destination mask non-empty, despite being unreachable.
This leaves migration with no usable destination because the source
node is not an eligible allocation target.

Restrict the hot-unplug migration target-list to N_MEMORY_COMMON so it
reflects the nodes the allocator can reach.

Signed-off-by: Gregory Price (Meta) <gourry@gourry.net>
---

2.56.1
View on lore ↗
Signals: acknowledged a fix is needed
Gregory Relay (author) ↳ replying to Gregory (Meta) Needs Work LLM
Author acknowledged that nodes outside N_MEMORY_COMMON cannot be generic demotion sources or destinations and agreed to modify the build demotion paths, default DRAM performance reference, and hotplug path to use only common memory nodes.
Show original comment 
From: "Gregory Price (Meta)" <gourry@gourry.net>

Memory outside N_MEMORY_COMMON is outside the ordinary allocation pool
and cannot be a generic demotion source or destination.

Build demotion paths and the default DRAM performance reference from
N_MEMORY_COMMON rather than every node that has memory, and do not
assign other nodes to a memory tier through the generic hotplug path.

Removing a node's memory already resets its demotion targets, so a node
that unplugs and returns outside N_MEMORY_COMMON keeps none.

Signed-off-by: Gregory Price (Meta) <gourry@gourry.net>
---

Last comment 2026-11-17 Re: [PATCH] mm/hmm: skip NUMA balancing on protnone faults Neutral LLM

Patch proposes to skip NUMA balancing on PROT_NONE faults, aiming to reduce GPU performance impact.

2 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Gregory Price (Meta) ↳ replying to Alistair Popple Neutral LLM
Raised questions about the performance implications of mmu_notifiers in the presence of PROT_NONE faults, specifically whether the driver needs a global view of memory or just a subset.
Signals: WAITING_FOR_REVIEW
Alistair Popple Positive LLM
Clarified the process of GPU page table setup and mmu_notifier synchronization, explaining that the driver doesn't need a global view of memory but rather just a view of the memory it needs to access.
Show original comment 
On 2026-11-17 at 10:57 +1100, "Gregory Price (Meta)" <gourry@gourry.net> wrote...
> On 2026-11-17 10:24 +1100, Alistair Popple wrote:
> > On 2026-11-14 at 07:53 +1100, "Gregory Price (Meta)" <gourry@gourry.net> wrote...
> > > On 2026-11-13 21:43 +0100, David Hildenbrand (Arm) wrote:
> > > > On 11/13/26 21:34, Thomas Hellstrm wrote:
> > > 
> > > > I recall that John (cc) kept saying "GPUs and NUMA balancing is inherently
> > > > incompatible, you have to disable NUMA balancing".
> > > > 
> > > 
> > > If this is a NUMA-hotplugged GPU, then this is resolved by a private
> > > node, because this:
> > > 
> > > @@ -6404,6 +6404,9 @@ static vm_fault_t do_numa_page(struct vm_fault *vmf)
> > >         if (!folio || folio_is_zone_device(folio))
> > > 
> > > 
> > > changes to
> > > 
> > >         if (!folio || folio_is_common_memory(folio))
> > > 
> > > And all folios on the GPU node are never subject to NUMA Balancing
> > > 
> > > so balancing can stay enabled while the GPU memory is not subject to
> > > PROT_NONE spew.
> > 
> > Hi Gregory, just to hopefully help clarify understanding a bit here this isn't
> > related to folio location and therefore private node won't help much.
> > 
> > This about a driver getting a snapshot of current PTE state to program a
> > secondary MMU (in this case on a GPU, but can equally be a NIC or other device).
> > 
> > Even CPU memory being subject to NUMA balancing can have quite an adverse impact
> > on GPU performance due to increased incidence of GPU faults, hence why John says
> > GPUs (using SVM) are inherently incompatible with NUMA balancing.
> > 
> 
> Ah, I follow.  I'm still working through the performance implications of
> mmu_notifiers in the presence of PROT_NONE faults, but if i'm
> understanding correctly it tracks that you'd say it's incompatible.

Incompatible is probably not the correct term as it still works. It's just that
GPU faults are really expensive, and to be avoided at all costs. So having NUMA
balancing running around randomly unmapping pages the GPU is using (regardless
of whether or not they are on the device) results in more GPU faults and
therefore a huge perf impact for basically no (or in fact sometimes negative)
gain.

> I presume here the driver needs a global view of memory (or at least all
> memory it can potentially DMA), rather than simply just a subset
> (some set of VMAs)?

The basic process is this:

* GPU needs to access some user space virtual address (eg. allocated with mmap/malloc)
* Therefore GPU driver needs to setup GPU page tables to point to the physical
  memory[1] backing the user space virtual address
* To find the physical address the GPU driver uses hmm_range_fault() on the
  range, possibly faulting pages in if required (ie. if CPU PTE permissions
  don't match what's required).
* hmm_range_fault() relies on mmu_notifiers for synchronisation with CPU PTE state.
* An mmu_notifier firing causes a driver to do two things:
  a) Must invalidate any GPU page tables / TLBs mapping the notified virtual address.
  b) Increments a sequence number for any concurrent hmm_range_faults(), which must
     retry as their view of CPU PTEs is now out-dated. This is the problem
     this patch series hits - the migration on fault causes an mmu_notifier to fire
     resulting in a hmm_range_fault() retry and associated overhead.

So the driver doesn't need a global view of memory, just a view of the memory
the GPU needs, or likely needs, to access. Determining that depends on the
driver - some might require pre-registration of the ranges (eg. via an ioctl)
prior to launching work whilst others might wait until the GPU work faults on an
address not in the GPU page tables. Or some combination thereof.

 - Alistair

[1] - Well technically the IOVA / DMA address mapping physical system memory
but that's not relevant for this discussion. We can assume there are no IOMMUs
present.

> ~Gregory
> 
> -- 
> ~Gregory
>

Last comment 2026-11-17 Re: [PATCH] mm/mempolicy: fix copy_nodes_to_user() for odd inputs Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch fixes a bug in the Linux kernel's memory policy module where the function copy_nodes_to_user() would read and write one extra byte from/to user space when maxnode - 1 is a multiple of 32, leading to a stack over-read and leak. The fix involves converting maxnode to a bit count on entry to copy_nodes_to_user() and get_nodes(), ensuring that both the native and compat paths get the same size for the node mask. This patch addresses an issue introduced in commit e130242dc351 and is intended to be backported to stable kernels.

1 reviewer comment — 0 participants
0 participants
Arnd Bergmann ↳ replying to Gregory (Meta) Inline Review Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Arnd Bergmann noted that renaming maxnode to nbits in copy_nodes_to_user() makes it harder to backport patches to stable kernels, and suggested doing a minimal fix first. Reviewer noted that the nbytes assignment in get_nodes() has no benefit for NODES_SHIFT >= 6 and suggested dropping it, assuming it was a mistake when introducing the bug.
Show original comment 
I like the renaming as a cleanup, though it does make it a little
harder to backport to stable kernels from context changes, maybe
it's better to do do the minimal fix first.

---

It fixes the bug, but I'd drop the nbytes= assignment here, as it
has no benefit: The only configurations where nbytes actually gets
a different value is for NODES_SHIFT<6, but as the on-stack
bitmask is still a 64-bit word here, the only difference is whether
the extra zeroes come from memset() or compat_put_bitmap().

I assume that when I introduced this bug, I simply confused which of
the two variables needed to be adjusted.

      Arnd

Please check this quick attempt with my codex. The patch count is reduced
to 52:

https://github.com/norov/linux/tree/private-nodes-v6-n-memory-folded

The brief summary for dropped/simplified patches is:

02    ->   dropped - State helpers: no publication/clearing helpers needed.
06\u201311 ->   dropped - Migration, tiers, DAMON, scheduler, stack locality:
           existing N_MEMORY checks already select ordinary memory.
14\u201315 ->   dropped - Hash distribution, kswapd recovery: existing N_MEMORY
           counts remain correct.
03 -> 02 - Common-memory state: documentation only; no new state or sysfs
           attribute.
05 -> 04 - Interleave/tmpfs policies: only the three-line weighted-interleave
           hotplug filter remains.
12 -> 05 - Reclaim: only hotplug kswapd gating remains; iterator conversion
           moves to the reclaim-state patch.
13 -> 06 - Compaction: only system-wide/proactive filtering and hotplug
           kcompactd gating remain.
16 -> 07 - OOM constraints: only capacity-summing intersections remain.
27 -> 18 - Private-memory state: expands to 16 files; topology/accounting
           users must include both memory sets.
31 -> 22 - Cpuset handling: requires ordinary memory while preserving
           allowed private nodes in the returned mask.
44 -> 36,
46 -> 38,
50 -> 42 - Service states: direct updates at boot,
           online, and offline sites replace shared helpers, increasing
           duplication.

The new N_MEMORY_NOALLOC patch is not split intentionally. If you do it, it
would probably turn into 4 individual patches.

The more important thing is that N_MEMORY and the proposed N_MEMORY_NOALLOC
is a disjoint, contrary to N_MEMORY and N_MEMORY_COMMON.
 
> And N_MEMORY_NOALLOC is not a good name.

Sure, I'm not attached to any particular name. Although,
N_MEMORY_COMMON nothing better to me. It answers the question
'what for this thing' even worse.

Thanks,
Yury

Show original comment 
On Mon, Nov 23, 2026 at 2:46 AM Gregory Price (Meta) <gourry@gourry.net> wrote:
>
> On 2026-11-22 13:42 +0100, David Hildenbrand (Arm) wrote:
> > Various callers (migration, memory-failure, s390_wiggle_split_folio) split
> > locked folios without holding a reference on the folio's inode.
> >
> > Splitting a file folio might allocate memory for the xarray and call
> > filemap_release_folio(). If either ends up in direct reclaim with
> > __GFP_FS, reclaim can evict the inode through the dcache shrinker, and
> > wait in truncate_inode_pages_final() for the folio that we hold locked:
> >
> >     try_split_folio()
> >       folio_lock(A)
> >       split_folio_to_list(A)
> >         xas_split_alloc()
> >           try_to_free_pages()
> >             super_cache_scan()
> >               prune_dcache_sb()
> >                 iput_final()
> >                   evict()
> >                     truncate_inode_pages_final()
> >                       folio_lock(A)
> >
> > This requires that nothing else holds a reference on the inode, and can
> > be triggered most easily with FUSE, which evicts inodes on the last
> > reference.
> >
> > Further, xas_split_alloc() charges the XArray nodes to the memcg, and on
> > cgroup v1, memcg reclaim waits for file folios under writeback if
> > __GFP_FS is set. That can deadlock if writeback waits for a folio lock
> > that we hold, for example with ext4.
> >
> > Let's fix it by splitting folios in a NOFS scope, so callers don't have
> > to care. A NOFS scope also covers allocations that filesystems make with
> > their own gfp flags, for example in filemap_release_folio().
> >
> > On NFS, splitting folios with pending writes or ongoing writes to
> > fscache might now fail slightly more often, until these writes have
> > completed. On netfs, only folios that are being written to fscache are
> > affected. All fairly irrelevant.
> >
> > This has medium urgency.
> >
> > Fixes: 6b24ca4a1a8d ("mm: Use multi-index entries in the page cache")
> > Fixes: eef88fe45ac9 ("s390/uv: Split large folios in gmap_make_secure()")
> > Fixes: 789763523fb4 ("xarray: honor XA_FLAGS_ACCOUNT in xas_split_alloc()")
> > Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org
> > Reported-by: Chris Mason <mason@kernel.org>
> > Signed-off-by: David Hildenbrand (Arm) <david@kernel.org>
>
> Reproduced the hang determnistically.
> Base deadlocked.
> With patch 1: -ENOMEM (safe)

Hi Gregory,

Just curious how those -ENOMEM failures occur. With GFP_NOFS, we
should still be able to reclaim anonymous folios (except in
SWAP_OPS_F_REQUIRE_NOFS cases) and clean page cache folios, right?

Last comment 2026-11-20 [PATCH] fs: use multigrain timestamps only with nanosecond granularity Neutral LLM

This patch modifies the multigrain timestamp behavior to only enable it when the superblock's time granularity is set to 1 nanosecond, ensuring that fine-grained timestamps are not truncated and can be accurately used for change detection. The patch also updates documentation to reflect this new requirement.

1 reviewer comment — 0 participants
0 participants
Jan Kara Reviewed-by Positive LLM
Jan Kara found no issues with the patch and suggested adding a Reviewed-by tag, indicating that he is satisfied with the changes but has not thoroughly reviewed them.
Show original comment 
On Thu 19-11-26 16:41:01, Jeff Layton wrote:
> Multigrain timestamps promise that a change after the ctime was
> queried shows a later ctime: inode_set_ctime_current() takes a
> fine-grained time when the coarse one isn't past the queried value.
> But it truncates that time to s_time_gran, and on a superblock with
> coarse granularity, such as ext4 with 128-byte inodes (one second),
> the truncated fine-grained time is no later than the coarse one, and
> the update is skipped. The inode is still flagged IOP_MGTIME, so a
> caller relying on is_mgtime() for that promise, such as nfsd deciding
> whether it can move the change attribute by moving ctime, is misled,
> and the multigrain machinery only costs a fine-grained clock read.
> 
> Set IOP_MGTIME only on a superblock whose s_time_gran is 1. Every
> FS_MGTIME filesystem (btrfs, ext4, f2fs, tmpfs, xfs) sets s_time_gran
> before it allocates an inode; document that it must.
> 
> Signed-off-by: Jeff Layton <jlayton@kernel.org>

Looks good. Feel free to add:

[PATCH RFC] NFS: don't wait for writeback on close() with a write delegation Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch modifies the NFS client to no longer wait for writeback on close() when a write delegation is held, reverting behavior introduced by commit 5445b1fbd123. The change was made because waiting for writeback on close() provided no benefits and could lead to issues such as delayed error reporting and unnecessary flushing of data. Instead, the patch returns to the previous behavior where writeback is started on close() only when a write delegation is held. This change improves performance in certain scenarios, specifically single-threaded create/write/close operations against an NFS server with a nonzero space limit.

[PATCH v2] NFS: don't wait for writeback on close() with a write delegation Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch modifies the NFS (Network File System) kernel code to no longer wait for writeback on close() when a write delegation is held. The previous behavior, introduced by commit 5445b1fbd123, caused servers that send a limit of 0 to not benefit from write delegations, even though they are granted by the server. The patch reverts this change and instead uses filemap_flush() for non-blocking writeback on close(). This resolves issues with error reporting and allows applications to rely on fsync() for flushing data to disk. The patch also removes unused code related to pagemod_limit.

Last comment 2026-11-19 [PATCH] NFSD: Reject a COPY whose source and destination are the same file Neutral LLM

This patch addresses a requirement in RFC 7862 that the NFSD server must reject COPY operations where the source and destination files are the same, regardless of range overlap. The existing code only checks for overlapping ranges, which is also required by CLONE operations. The patch adds a check to nfsd4_setup_intra_ssc() to verify if the source and destination file handles refer to the same file.

Show original comment 
nfsd_break_deleg_cb() can run more than once per lease:
- __break_lease() calls lm_break for a read breaker
  (FL_DOWNGRADE_PENDING) and again for a later write breaker
  (FL_UNLOCK_PENDING);
- nfsd_handle_dir_event() and nfsd_recall_all_dir_delegs() call it
  directly, possibly repeatedly.

Each call incremented cl_delegs_in_recall, but nfsd_change_deleg_cb()
decrements it once at F_UNLCK. The leaked count keeps
nfs4_anylock_blockers() true, so the client can never again become a
courtesy client.

Skip setting dl_recalled, the increment, the client expiry, and
fi_had_conflict when dl_recalled is already set. All callers and the
F_UNLCK decrement run under flc_lock. fl_break_time = 0,
nfsd_break_one_deleg() and the false return stay unconditional.

nfs4_open_delegation() refuses delegations for NFSv4.0 write opens on
CLAIM_NULL and CLAIM_FH, as its comment explains, but CLAIM_PREVIOUS
skips the check. An NFSv4.0 client reclaiming a write open during grace

Jeff Layton Inline Review Positive LLM
Addressed a bug where the NFSv4 server was granting read delegations with attributes when timestamps were requested, leading to incorrect behavior. Changed the delegation type to OPEN_DELEGATE_READ instead.
Show original comment 
__nfsd_setattr() adds ATTR_CTIME only when ATTR_DELEG is clear, to keep a
delegated atime-only update from moving ctime. But ATTR_DELEG is set for
the whole iattr, so a holder's chmod or chown sent along with a delegated
atime changes the mode or owner without updating ctime.

Add ATTR_CTIME unless the request is a delegated atime-only update. A
delegated mtime whose ctime failed vetting now gets ctime = now instead
of no ctime update.

Jeff Layton (author) Positive Heuristic
When a topology change event finds a busy device, _scsih_fw_event_requeue() retries the event. It queues the event's delayed_work on firmware_event_thread, with a one-second delay. _scsih_fw_event_cleanup_queue() cancels only the event's work, not its delayed_work. Thus the timer stays armed. Then scsih_remove() destroys the workqueue. After the workqueue is freed, the timer queues _firmware_event_work_delayed() on it: BUG: KASAN: slab-use-after-free in __queue_work+0xa12/0xb50 Read of size 4 at addr ffff88814fefd1c0 by task swapper/10/0 __queue_work call_timer_fn __run_timer_base ... Allocated by task 11: __alloc_workqueue alloc_workqueue_noprof _scsih_probe [mpt3sas] Freed by task 0: kfree rcu_core ...
Show original comment 
When a topology change event finds a busy device,
_scsih_fw_event_requeue() retries the event. It queues the event's
delayed_work on firmware_event_thread, with a one-second delay.
_scsih_fw_event_cleanup_queue() cancels only the event's work, not its
delayed_work. Thus the timer stays armed. Then scsih_remove() destroys
the workqueue. After the workqueue is freed, the timer queues
_firmware_event_work_delayed() on it:

  BUG: KASAN: slab-use-after-free in __queue_work+0xa12/0xb50
  Read of size 4 at addr ffff88814fefd1c0 by task swapper/10/0
   __queue_work
   call_timer_fn
   __run_timer_base
   ...
  Allocated by task 11:
   __alloc_workqueue
   alloc_workqueue_noprof
   _scsih_probe [mpt3sas]
  Freed by task 0:
   kfree
   rcu_core
  ...
  workqueue: cannot queue _firmware_event_work_delayed [mpt3sas] on wq fw_event_mpt3sas0

The cleanup does not wait for a running topology change event. If such
an event finds a busy device, it requeues itself. This can occur after
the cleanup passes the event.

Fix this:

 - In the cleanup, cancel the delayed_work of a requeued event.

 - Do not requeue an event while the cleanup runs or while the driver
   removes the host. Do this check under fw_event_lock, which the
   cleanup also takes.

 - Instead of a requeue, drop the reference that the work held.
   Previously, this reference leaked when firmware_event_thread was
   NULL.

 - Also drop the reference if the delayed_work cannot be queued.
   Cleanups can overlap. Thus one cleanup can clear fw_events_cleanup
   while another cleanup cancels the delayed_work. The cancel disables
   the delayed_work.

 - Initialize the delayed_work when the driver allocates the event, not
   at the first requeue. Thus a cleanup never cancels the delayed_work
   before it is initialized.

Fixes: ad5957193107 ("scsi: mpt3sas: Add firmware event requeue support for busy devices")

2.56.1
View on lore ↗
Signals: queued
Jeff Layton (author) Positive Heuristic
_scsih_fw_event_cleanup_queue() uses events without a reference to them. But the work of an event frees the event when the work ends. This causes these problems: - dequeue_next_fw_event() drops the list's reference before the cleanup cancels the event's work. If the work runs and ends between these two steps, the cleanup calls cancel_work_sync() on a freed event. - The cleanup holds no reference to an event that it finds through ioc->current_event. The worker drops its reference before it clears current_event. Thus the cleanup can find a freed event there, and set ->ignore on it or read its ->event. For a running MPT3SAS_REMOVE_UNRESPONDING_DEVICES event, cancel_work_sync() waits for the work. The work frees the event, and then cancel_work_sync() enables the work again in freed memory.
Show original comment 
_scsih_fw_event_cleanup_queue() uses events without a reference to
them. But the work of an event frees the event when the work ends. This
causes these problems:

 - dequeue_next_fw_event() drops the list's reference before the
   cleanup cancels the event's work. If the work runs and ends between
   these two steps, the cleanup calls cancel_work_sync() on a freed
   event.

 - The cleanup holds no reference to an event that it finds through
   ioc->current_event. The worker drops its reference before it clears
   current_event. Thus the cleanup can find a freed event there, and
   set ->ignore on it or read its ->event. For a running
   MPT3SAS_REMOVE_UNRESPONDING_DEVICES event, cancel_work_sync() waits
   for the work. The work frees the event, and then cancel_work_sync()
   enables the work again in freed memory.

 - To identify a running event, the cleanup only compares the event
   with current_event. It waits for the work of all other events that
   it finds. But the cleanup also clears current_event. Also, cleanups
   can overlap, because _base_fault_reset_work() calls a cleanup
   without reset_in_progress_mutex. Thus a cleanup that holds
   reset_in_progress_mutex can wait for a running event. That event is
   about to take the same mutex for a hard reset.

Originally, commit 146b16c8071f ("mpt3sas: Refcount fw_events and fix
unsafe list usage") kept the list's reference until after the cancel.
Then commit 9e73ed2e4cf5 ("scsi: mpt3sas: Cancel the running work
during host reset") added the current_event path. Events on this path
had no reference to drop. To fix the resulting underflow, commit
991df3dd5144 ("scsi: mpt3sas: Fix use-after-free warning") dropped the
list's reference early.

Fix the reference handling:

 - Let dequeue_next_fw_event() pass the list's reference to the cleanup
   again. The cleanup drops the reference after the cancel.

 - Set and clear current_event under fw_event_lock. Clear it before the
   worker drops its reference. Then the cleanup can take a reference to
   the current event under the lock.

 - When the worker requeues the event, clear current_event in
   _scsih_fw_event_requeue(). Do this under fw_event_lock, together
   with the requeue or the drop of the event. _scsih_fw_event_requeue()
   can now drop the reference.

 - Also do the check for events to clean up under the lock.

Thus the cleanup finds the event either as the current event or on
fw_event_list. It cannot miss the event between the two.

When the worker starts an event, remove the event from fw_event_list
and make it the current event. Do both in one step under
fw_event_lock. If the event is no longer on the list, a cleanup removed
it, so drop it. Thus an event that the cleanup removes from the list
never runs. The cancel of its work then never waits for a running
event.

Do not change current_event in the cleanup. Let the worker manage it.
Wait for the running event only if it is
MPT3SAS_REMOVE_UNRESPONDING_DEVICES, as before.

Only the broadcast primitive handler clears broadcast_aen_busy. If the
cleanup drops a broadcast primitive event, broadcast_aen_busy stays
set. Later broadcast primitives then only count as pending. An event
that the cleanup cancels before it runs already causes this. With this
change, an event that the worker drops as it starts also causes it.
When the cleanup drops such an event, clear broadcast_aen_busy and the
stale pending count.

Fixes: 9e73ed2e4cf5 ("scsi: mpt3sas: Cancel the running work during host reset")
Fixes: 991df3dd5144 ("scsi: mpt3sas: Fix use-after-free warning")
Signed-off-by: Jeff Layton <jlayton@kernel.org>

2.56.1
View on lore ↗
Signals: queued
Jeff Layton (author) Neutral Heuristic
Since commit ad5957193107 ("scsi: mpt3sas: Add firmware event requeue support for busy devices"), _scsih_sas_topology_change_event() requeues the event whenever _scsih_add_device() returns non-zero. It retries the event every second. But _scsih_add_device() returns 1 only for a busy device. It returns -1 when it cannot add the device at all. This occurs when: - the driver cannot read the SAS Device Page 0 of the device - the device is not an end device, or is not present - discovery reported an access problem - the device is already added None of these conditions clear with time, and nothing limits the number of retries. In a stress test, the driver retried the add of a device that was not present 249 times. A requeued event keeps the handle and phy number from its original report.
Show original comment 
Since commit ad5957193107 ("scsi: mpt3sas: Add firmware event requeue
support for busy devices"), _scsih_sas_topology_change_event() requeues
the event whenever _scsih_add_device() returns non-zero. It retries the
event every second. But _scsih_add_device() returns 1 only for a busy
device. It returns -1 when it cannot add the device at all. This occurs
when:

 - the driver cannot read the SAS Device Page 0 of the device
 - the device is not an end device, or is not present
 - discovery reported an access problem
 - the device is already added

None of these conditions clear with time, and nothing limits the
number of retries. In a stress test, the driver retried the add of a
device that was not present 249 times.

A requeued event keeps the handle and phy number from its original
report. Meanwhile, the handle may go to another device, because a host
reset assigns all handles again. In one stress run, a requeued add
first ran after a host reset. At that time, its handle was not in use.
Thus the read of SAS Device Page 0 failed, and the driver requeued the
event. One second later, at the retry, a device that was returning on
another phy had that handle.

The retry then makes mpt3sas_transport_update_links() record the SAS
address of that device on the phy of the event. The add of the device
then makes _transport_sanity_check() delete the port of the drive on
that phy. Thus the driver removed the drive, although the drive never
disappeared.

When _scsih_add_device() cannot add the device, return 0, as
_scsih_pcie_add_device() does. Then the driver retries only a busy
device. Some failures of the page read do clear with time:

 - -EAGAIN, when no internal command is free or after a reset
 - an IOC status of BUSY or INSUFFICIENT_RESOURCES

Continue to retry these failures, up to command_retry_count times, as
for a busy device. This change also prevents a spin on such a device
in the loops that call _scsih_add_device() after a host reset.

Fixes: ad5957193107 ("scsi: mpt3sas: Add firmware event requeue support for busy devices")
Signed-off-by: Jeff Layton <jlayton@kernel.org>

2.56.1
View on lore ↗
Jeff Layton (author) Neutral Heuristic
_scsih_ublock_io_device_wait() gets the pcie_device on each pass of its TEST UNIT READY retry loop. But it puts the pcie_device only at the bottom of the loop. The DEVICE_RETRY/START_UNIT/STOP_UNIT/RETRY_UA branch ends in a continue, which skips the put. Thus each retry of an NVMe device leaks a reference, and the driver can never free the _pcie_device. The function needs the pcie_device only for the reset timeout and the reset method. Put the pcie_device immediately after the function reads these values. --- drivers/scsi/mpt3sas/mpt3sas_scsih.
Show original comment 
_scsih_ublock_io_device_wait() gets the pcie_device on each pass of its
TEST UNIT READY retry loop. But it puts the pcie_device only at the
bottom of the loop. The DEVICE_RETRY/START_UNIT/STOP_UNIT/RETRY_UA
branch ends in a continue, which skips the put. Thus each retry of an
NVMe device leaks a reference, and the driver can never free the
_pcie_device.

The function needs the pcie_device only for the reset timeout and the
reset method. Put the pcie_device immediately after the function reads
these values.

2.56.1
View on lore ↗
Jeff Layton (author) Neutral Heuristic
When the firmware reports that a device responds again, the event worker unblocks the sdevs of the device. Previously, the worker used _scsih_ublock_io_device() for this, which uses _scsih_internal_device_unblock(). The unblock can fail with -EINVAL, if the SD layer set the device to SDEV_RUNNING while our block still stopped its queue. Then that helper blocks the device again and unblocks it, to restart the queue. _scsih_ublock_io_device_wait(), which does a TUR check, replaced _scsih_ublock_io_device().
Show original comment 
When the firmware reports that a device responds again, the event
worker unblocks the sdevs of the device. Previously, the worker used
_scsih_ublock_io_device() for this, which uses
_scsih_internal_device_unblock(). The unblock can fail with -EINVAL, if
the SD layer set the device to SDEV_RUNNING while our block still
stopped its queue. Then that helper blocks the device again and
unblocks it, to restart the queue.

_scsih_ublock_io_device_wait(), which does a TUR check, replaced
_scsih_ublock_io_device(). It open-codes the unblock without that
retry, so such a device can stay stalled. Use
_scsih_internal_device_unblock() in _scsih_ublock_io_device_wait() too.

2.56.1
View on lore ↗
Jeff Layton (author) Neutral Heuristic
_scsih_ublock_io_all_device() and _scsih_ublock_io_device_wait() poll each blocked sdev with TEST UNIT READY, for up to command_retry_count retries. These functions set the retry counter to 0 once per call, not once per sdev. Thus retries on one device consume the retry budget of all later devices. When the budget is empty, the driver marks a device deleted after its first TUR and sets it SDEV_OFFLINE. This occurs even for a device that needs only one retry. Start the count from 0 for each sdev. --- drivers/scsi/mpt3sas/mpt3sas_scsih.c | 6 ++++-- 1 file changed, 4 insertions(+), 2 deletions(-) diff --git a/drivers/scsi/mpt3sas/mpt3sas_scsih.c b/drivers/scsi/mpt3sas/mpt3sas_scsih.
Show original comment 
_scsih_ublock_io_all_device() and _scsih_ublock_io_device_wait() poll
each blocked sdev with TEST UNIT READY, for up to command_retry_count
retries. These functions set the retry counter to 0 once per call, not
once per sdev. Thus retries on one device consume the retry budget of
all later devices. When the budget is empty, the driver marks a device
deleted after its first TUR and sets it SDEV_OFFLINE. This occurs even
for a device that needs only one retry.

Start the count from 0 for each sdev.

2.56.1
View on lore ↗
Jeff Layton (author) Neutral Heuristic
When the missing_delay module parameter is set, mpt3sas_base_update_missing_delay() writes the new delays to SAS IO Unit Page 1. If the write succeeds, the function caches the requested values in ioc->device_missing_delay and ioc->io_missing_delay. mpt3sas_config_set_sas_iounit_pg1() ignores the result of the WRITE_CURRENT request. It returns the result of the subsequent WRITE_NVRAM request. If the WRITE_CURRENT request fails and the WRITE_NVRAM request succeeds, the cached delays do not match the delays that the IOC uses. The device_delay sysfs attribute then reports the wrong value. After the write, read the page again and cache the delays that it reports. If this read fails, cache the requested delays if the NVRAM update succeeded, as before. --- drivers/scsi/mpt3sas/mpt3sas_base.
Show original comment 
When the missing_delay module parameter is set,
mpt3sas_base_update_missing_delay() writes the new delays to SAS IO
Unit Page 1. If the write succeeds, the function caches the requested
values in ioc->device_missing_delay and ioc->io_missing_delay.

mpt3sas_config_set_sas_iounit_pg1() ignores the result of the
WRITE_CURRENT request. It returns the result of the subsequent
WRITE_NVRAM request. If the WRITE_CURRENT request fails and the
WRITE_NVRAM request succeeds, the cached delays do not match the delays
that the IOC uses. The device_delay sysfs attribute then reports the
wrong value.

After the write, read the page again and cache the delays that it
reports. If this read fails, cache the requested delays if the NVRAM
update succeeded, as before.

2.56.1
View on lore ↗
Jeff Layton (author) Neutral Heuristic
On DELAY_NOT_RESPONDING, the event callback runs _scsih_block_io_device() at interrupt time. This function walks the sdevs with shost_for_each_device(). It uses sdev->hostdata and its sas_target, and holds no lock. The sdev reference pins only the scsi_device. __scsi_remove_device() can still call ->sdev_destroy() at the same time, for example when a scan fails to probe a new LUN. scsih_sdev_destroy() frees hostdata, which causes a use-after-free. scsi_target_reap() frees the hostdata of the target, after sdev_destroy. Add ioc->block_lock. Take it: - in _scsih_block_io_device(), for each sdev - around the code that sets and clears sdev->hostdata in scsih_sdev_init() and scsih_sdev_destroy() The sdev is on shost->__devices before sdev_init.
Show original comment 
On DELAY_NOT_RESPONDING, the event callback runs
_scsih_block_io_device() at interrupt time. This function walks the
sdevs with shost_for_each_device(). It uses sdev->hostdata and its
sas_target, and holds no lock.

The sdev reference pins only the scsi_device. __scsi_remove_device()
can still call ->sdev_destroy() at the same time, for example when a
scan fails to probe a new LUN. scsih_sdev_destroy() frees hostdata,
which causes a use-after-free. scsi_target_reap() frees the hostdata of
the target, after sdev_destroy.

Add ioc->block_lock. Take it:

 - in _scsih_block_io_device(), for each sdev
 - around the code that sets and clears sdev->hostdata in
   scsih_sdev_init() and scsih_sdev_destroy()

The sdev is on shost->__devices before sdev_init. Because the driver
publishes hostdata under the lock, the lock also orders the sas_target
store. While the lock is held and hostdata is non-NULL, hostdata and
its target are live.

Jeff Layton (author) Neutral Heuristic
After a hard reset, the MPT3SAS_REMOVE_UNRESPONDING_DEVICES event polls each blocked device with TEST UNIT READY in _scsih_ublock_io_all_device(). It retries a device for up to command_retry_count seconds (144 by default). If another hard reset starts during this time, it sets shost_recovery. Then _scsih_fw_event_cleanup_queue() waits for the event in cancel_work_sync(). While shost_recovery is set, _scsih_wait_for_device_to_become_ready() returns DEVICE_RETRY and does not test the device. Thus, before the reset can continue, the event consumes all the retries of the device that it polls. The event then marks that device deleted and offline, although it never tested the device. If the event is cancelled, or if the host is being removed, the event already returns before it polls.
Show original comment 
After a hard reset, the MPT3SAS_REMOVE_UNRESPONDING_DEVICES event polls
each blocked device with TEST UNIT READY in
_scsih_ublock_io_all_device(). It retries a device for up to
command_retry_count seconds (144 by default).

If another hard reset starts during this time, it sets shost_recovery.
Then _scsih_fw_event_cleanup_queue() waits for the event in
cancel_work_sync(). While shost_recovery is set,
_scsih_wait_for_device_to_become_ready() returns DEVICE_RETRY and does
not test the device. Thus, before the reset can continue, the event
consumes all the retries of the device that it polls. The event then
marks that device deleted and offline, although it never tested the
device.

If the event is cancelled, or if the host is being removed, the event
already returns before it polls. Do the same check during polling, and
stop polling if the check is true. The remaining devices stay blocked.
The reset queues a new MPT3SAS_REMOVE_UNRESPONDING_DEVICES event, which
polls them after recovery ends. If the host is removed, the removal
deletes them.

Fixes: 72340fecd0c8 ("scsi: mpt3sas: Add configurable command retry limit for slow-to-respond devices")
Signed-off-by: Jeff Layton <jlayton@kernel.org>
