+/**
+ * _scsih_unresponding_scan_cancelled - should polling devices stop
+ * @ioc: per adapter object
+ *
+ * While the MPT3SAS_REMOVE_UNRESPONDING_DEVICES event polls devices, a
+ * hard reset or the removal of the host waits for it to finish, and the
+ * readiness check doesn't test devices during a reset.
+ *
+ * Return: true if the event is being cancelled, false otherwise.
+ */
+static bool
+_scsih_unresponding_scan_cancelled(struct MPT3SAS_ADAPTER *ioc)
+{
+	return READ_ONCE(ioc->remove_host) || READ_ONCE(ioc->fw_events_cleanup);
+}
+
 /**
  * _scsih_ublock_io_all_device - unblock every device
  * @ioc: per adapter object
@@ -4114,7 +4130,13 @@ _scsih_ublock_io_all_device(struct MPT3SAS_ADAPTER *ioc, u8 no_turs)
 				pcie_device_put(pcie_device);
 		} while ((rc == DEVICE_RETRY || rc == DEVICE_START_UNIT ||
 		    rc == DEVICE_STOP_UNIT || rc == DEVICE_RETRY_UA)
-			&& count++ < command_retry_count);
+			&& count++ < command_retry_count &&
+			!_scsih_unresponding_scan_cancelled(ioc));
+		if (_scsih_unresponding_scan_cancelled(ioc)) {
+			/* a reset queues a new scan, which polls the rest */
+			scsi_device_put(sdev);
+			return;
+		}
 		sas_device_priv_data->block = 0;
 		if (rc != DEVICE_READY)
 			sas_device_priv_data->deleted = 1;

> Changes in v3:
> - New patches 1-8 and 10 fix pre-existing bugs in the code that this
>   series changes:
>   - 1-2: fix UAFs and refcounting in the cleanup of the fw event queue.
>     Do not leave requeued events pending at teardown.
>   - 3: requeue topology events only for busy devices. Do not requeue
>     them for devices that the driver cannot add.
>   - 4-7: fix these TUR polling bugs:
>     - a pcie_device leak
>     - a lost unblock retry
>     - polling that continues after the scan is cancelled
>     - a retry count that is not per device
>   - 8: cache the missing delays that the IOC actually uses
>   - 10: do not use freed hostdata while the driver polls devices
> - Patch 9: also publish sdev->hostdata under ioc->block_lock in
>   scsih_sdev_init()
> - Patch 11:
>   - Use the PCIe missing delay (from PCIe IO Unit Page 1) for NVMe
>     devices.
>   - Arm dnr_unblock_work for the earliest deadline.
>   - Skip the timed unblock during host reset, but not during SCSI EH.
>   - Clear the deadline of an sdev under ioc->block_lock before the fw
>     event worker polls the sdev.
>   - Do not put a timed block on offline sdevs.
>   - When a hard reset cancels polling, give new deadlines to the sdevs
>     that stay blocked.
>   - Give deadlines to the sdevs that a broadcast primitive pass blocks.
>     Prevent dnr_unblock_work from unblocking them while the pass runs.
>   - A repeat DNR restarts the deadline, at most
>     MPT3SAS_DNR_BLOCK_MAX_EXTENDS times.
>   - At the next worker unblock, do a TUR check on expired sdevs.
>   - Keep the blocks that the driver makes during polling.
>   - Use system_dfl_wq instead of system_wq.

> - New patch 1: use a new ioc->block_lock to serialize the
>   interrupt-time sdev block with the hostdata teardown in
>   scsih_sdev_destroy(). This fixes a pre-existing UAF.
> - Do the timed unblock under ioc->block_lock. This prevents a race with
>   an interrupt-time block.

>   device with TURs. Then dnr_unblock_work cannot unblock the device

Last comment 2026-11-18 Re: [RFC PATCH v4 00/10] add provenance time (ptime) Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series proposes to add a 'provenance time' (ptime) field to the Linux kernel, which would track when an inode's metadata was last updated. However, it seems that managing this field in the kernel is complex and may require userland intervention. A simpler approach suggested by Jeff Layton is to make the existing 'btime' (birth time) settable from userland or to store the provenance time as an extended attribute (xattr).

1 reviewer comment — 0 participants
0 participants
koraynilay ↳ replying to Jeff Layton Inline Review Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer noted that managing provenance time (ptime) in the kernel is challenging and suggested revisiting the decision to make btime settable from userland, or alternatively tracking ptime as an xattr. Reviewer koraynilay clarified the meaning of various inode timestamps, explaining that btime is 'inode birth/creation time', ctime is 'inode change time', mtime is 'file content change/modification time', and ptime would be 'file content creation time'. Reviewer noted that setting an immutable inode creation time (ctime) is necessary even if btime is made settable, and suggested tracking the new timestamp in an xattr instead of adding a new per-inode field. Reviewer noted that userland xattrs have limitations (e.g., don't work on symlinks) and suggested using them instead of adding a new inode timestamp, citing their own experience with fully userspace xattrs for backups. reviewer expressed concerns that the proposed solution to track provenance time is overly complex and suggested a simpler alternative of tracking it in an xattr, citing samba's behavior as an example
Show original comment 
Hello and thanks for replying,

On Wed Nov 18, 2026 at 12:53 AM CET, Jeff Layton wrote:

---

|-----\_-----------------|-----------|
  |       \_      change/  |   birth/  |
  |         \_ modification|  creation |
  |           \------------|-----------|
  | inode time |   ctime   |   btime   |
  |------------|-----------|-----------|
  | file time  |   mtime   |   ptime   |
  |------------|-----------|-----------|
  
("file" as in "the concept of this file", I'd say "file content" but
that would be incorrect for symlinks)

so ctime is "inode change time", btime is "inode birth/creation time",
while mtime is "file content change/modification time" and ptime would
be "file content creation time" (same as Window's or MacOS's creation
time).

Does this make more sense?

---

Originally I just wanted to have btime settable, yes, and if btime was
settable I wouldn't have made this RFC PATCH either, but after reading
the already existing discussions about it, I can understand having an
"immutable inode creation time", like ctime exists, so even after making
btime settable, a new timestamp would be necessary anyway (e.g. for the
people who want btime to exist as a forensics timestamp).

---

(This is copied and adapted from [2])

I actually have been using fully userspace xattrs[1] for a few months,
mostly for backups, and it "works"; but last month I wanted to merge 2
FSs (and have another merge I have to do), so I ran [1] on all files and
then compared all metadata/data between source and destination and
noticed there were quite a lot of files that didn't have the xattr set.
So I looked online and learned that xattrs only work on regular
files/directories, not on symlinks or anything else (and the missing
ones were of symlinks).
This is what prompted me to search more if anyone had already proposed a
new timestamp (instead of making btime settable) and found the email
thread from april linked in the cover letter, which gave me the excuse
to finally work on a proper patch.

---

So yeah while I do agree it touches a lot of code that could create
problems, IMO this is the best solution and it would complete files'
timestamps like other OSs have (and this, I think, is necessary for
both backup/archival and migrations).

Thanks,
koraynilay

[1]: https://github.com/koraynilay/btime-to-xattr
[2]: https://lore.kernel.org/DMGS4KQLY24Q.14MUTR6U93GIC@gmail.com/

Patches Submitted (3)
Ongoing Submitted 2026-11-09 🔥 TODAY [PATCH v1] mm/rmap: fix migration entries being treated as partial unmaps Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch fixes an issue where migration entries in the Linux kernel are being treated as partial unmaps, leading to incorrect behavior such as unnecessary folio splits and overcounting of split_deferred statistics. The problem occurs when a large anonymous folio is migrated or split, causing the PG_partially_mapped flag to be set even though no actual unmapping has taken place. To fix this, the patch introduces a new flag that prevents __folio_remove_rmap() from marking a folio as partially mapped when migration entries are replaced for PTEs. This ensures that only actual unmappings trigger the deferred split queue and corrects the statistics. The patch also addresses issues related to migrated folios being queued for unnecessary splits and overcounting of split_deferred statistics.

9 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
David (Arm) ↳ replying to Joanne Koong Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer noted that the patch addresses a similar issue he raised last year, where THPs were being incorrectly marked as partially mapped after migration or splitting, and suggested that the current patch's solution is an improvement because it copies the state to the new folio, allowing for correct splitting. Reviewer David noted that the patch does not address all possible scenarios and asked if the issue also occurs when using try_to_unmap(), pointing out a potential oversight. Reviewer David (Arm) expressed strong disagreement with the patch's approach, suggesting that instead of adding a 'magical boolean parameter', the rmap flags should be passed directly for another purpose. He also pointed out that the issue can be resolved by batching unmap operations in the common case and checking for partial mapping in the deferred split handler.

This is a similar problem I raised in last year's LSF/MM:

"
2. THPs erroneously detected as partially-mapped
 * Failing migration/split/eviction after stepwise unmap

The news here is that we now copy the state to the new folio and can

We will not add such magical boolean parameters. I have a patch to just pass the

You should probably look at

commit 354dffd29575cdf13154e8fb787322354aa9efc4
Author: Barry Song <baohua@kernel.org>
Date:   Fri Feb 14 22:30:14 2025 +1300

    mm: support batched unmap for lazyfree large folios during reclamation

    Currently, the PTEs and rmap of a large folio are removed one at a time.
    This is not only slow but also causes the large folio to be unnecessarily
    added to deferred_split, which can lead to races between the
    deferred_split shrinker callback and memory reclamation.  This patch
    releases all PTEs and rmap entries in a batch.  Currently, it only handles
    lazyfree large folios.

Batching resolves the problem in the common case. For the remaining parts, we
should likely just check in the deferred split handler whether actually
partially mapped to then clear the flag if not.

-- 
Cheers,

David

On Tue, Nov 10, 2026 at 3:49 AM David Hildenbrand (Arm)
<david@kernel.org> wrote:

---

As I understand it, there's no simple way to check if a folio is
partially mapped or not under CONFIG_NO_PAGE_MAPCOUNT without doing a
rmap walk. Would it make sense to do something simpler here instead
where we just check the folio's partially mapped flag and cache that
before we replace the PTEs with migration entries, and then for those
edge cases that the batching solution doesn't cover, just restore that
flag back to its original state?

I think this would add coverage for the migration and reclaim paths,
instead of just the deferred split shrinker, but would miss the race
where someone tries to do a partial unmap while the folio has the
migration PTEs, but I think this is already a race that exists in the
batching case. imo it seems acceptable though given that the race is
pretty rare and doesn't have any adverse effects except delaying
freeing the unmapped pages until the whole folio gets unmapped.

Do you think something like this makes sense, or would you prefer
having the check in the deferred split handler?

Thanks,
Joanne

Neutral LLM

This patch series aims to optimize the reading of memcg stats by using global counters instead of flushing, which is expensive and can lead to stale statistics. The root memcg's stats are updated directly from the node counters for most metrics, while a few remaining cases will be addressed in subsequent patches.

Ongoing Submitted 2026-11-03 Last comment 2026-11-17 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH v4 0/4] mm: split underused anonymous mTHP folios 4 patches
Show 4 individual patches
  • [1/4] mm/huge_memory: scale thp_underused() to the folio size Neutral Submitted 2026-11-03Last comment 2026-11-17 Kiryl Shutsemau Zi Yan Baolin Wang Joanne Koong (author) David (Arm)
  • [2/4] mm/huge_memory: don't queue folios when underused splitting is inactive Neutral Submitted 2026-11-03Last comment 2026-11-10 Usama Arif Kiryl Shutsemau Baolin Wang
  • [3/4] mm/huge_memory: add a per-order counter for underused splits Neutral Submitted 2026-11-03Last comment 2026-11-17 Usama Arif Kiryl Shutsemau Joanne Koong (author) Baolin Wang David (Arm)
  • [4/4] mm: add anonymous mTHP folios to the deferred split list Neutral Submitted 2026-11-03Last comment 2026-11-17 Barry Song David (Arm)
Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series addresses memory regressions caused by 2M THP=always on arm64 by allowing the underused shrinker to handle anonymous mTHP folios. The patches modify thp_underused() to be folio-size-aware, stop queueing folios for underused splitting when khugepaged/max_ptes_none is at its default maximum, and add a per-order split_underused counter. Finally, it queues anonymous mTHP folios on the deferred split list with a minimum size of 2M.

Patches Submitted (5)
Last comment 2026-11-19 [PATCH] drm/i915/gem: Skip writeback of shmem folios with extra references Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch modifies the drm/i915/gem module to skip writeback of shmem folios that have extra references, preventing a race condition where a stale TLB entry can still store data to an unmapped folio until the TLB is flushed. The change ensures that such folios are left to reclaim as mapped ones would be, rather than being written back from swap without the stored data.

2 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Andi Shyti ↳ replying to Kiryl (Meta) Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM

Reviewer Andi Shyti pointed out that the patch is unnecessary because the i915 writeback iterator already skips shmem folios in the described case, making the new refcount check redundant.

---

writeback iterator already skips the shmem folio in the case you
describe, so the new refcount check doesn't change the outcome.

Andi

Last comment 2026-11-17 [PATCH] mm/compaction: do not skip the free scanner's periodic lock drop Neutral LLM

This patch modifies the isolate_freepages_block function in mm/compaction.c to fix a bug where the free scanner's periodic lock drop was being skipped when scanning pageblocks with strides above one. The issue arises because the scan steps over compound pages or high-order free pages, leading to potential deadlocks. To address this, the patch counts probes instead of pfns and checks every COMPACT_CLUSTER_MAX of them.

2 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants

On Mon, 16 Nov 2026 12:57:45 +0000 "Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta)" <kas@kernel.org> wrote:

Signals: QUESTION
Ongoing Submitted 2026-11-04 Last comment 2026-11-16 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH] mm/compaction: sample empty pageblocks again from a random offset Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch modifies the Linux kernel's compaction algorithm to sample empty pageblocks from a random offset when the initial scan finds nothing. This is done to prevent aliasing issues that can cause free pages to be missed, particularly in sparse layouts or when memory is not aligned with the sampling grid. The change aims to improve the detection of free pages and reduce the likelihood of compaction failures, which can lead to stalled tasks and inefficient memory usage.

4 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Dev Jain ↳ replying to Kiryl (Meta) Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Dev Jain questioned whether the patch's benefits would be observable in real workloads, citing low probabilities of missing free pages at random offsets and potential for unnecessary scans over fully filled memory.
Show original comment 
That is where it gets hairy: will this be observable in real workloads?

Suppose stride is 32 and the pageblock has 100 non-free pages. What is
the probability that 512 slots are filled with 100 objects such that
x = 0 (mod 32) is filled? It is

C(496, 84) / C(512, 100) = 1.6 *10^(-12), very improbable.

So the chance you have a sparse block and you are missing out by not
looking at other offsets is very low.

If stride = 32 and #objects = 400, the probability comes out to be
1.8%.

Keeping #objects fixed, lowering the stride lowers the probability
exponentially. (This is relevant since we start with lower strides)

On the other hand, what happens if you are scanning over fully filled
memory? (there is no way to tell whether a pageblock is sparse)
You are going to repeat the scans unnecessarily.

IOW I think the probability this patch is going to have a downside is
more than that of upside.
View on lore ↗
Signals: probability, downside
Vlastimil (SUSE) ↳ replying to Dev Jain Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Vlastimil requested that the patch provide a real-life motivation for the change, as the current implementation appears to be based on an artificially constructed scenario.
Show original comment 
Agreed. This seems to all be based on a hypothetical scenario, only
constructed artificially to demonstrate it. A real-life motivation is needed.
View on lore ↗
Signals: requested changes
Kiryl (Meta) (author) ↳ replying to Vlastimil (SUSE) Neutral LLM
Author acknowledged that their test case was not representative of real-world scenarios, but explained it was based on a bisection to identify the root cause of an issue in another series.
Show original comment 
Fair enough, let's drop it.

It is not entirely made up, though.

I stepped on it while testing another series. A fragmentation stress
test gave bimodal results: no direct reclaim in most runs and hundreds
to thousands of allocstalls in others.

It was confusing and looked like a regression in that series.

It took a bisection to find that the only thing that mattered was where
the free pages fell relative to the stride grid. The test in the
changelog was built after that, to show it on purpose.

The workload is very artificial, but the fix looked simple enough to
post.

-- 
  Kiryl Shutsemau / Kirill A. Shutemov

Show original comment 
Yeah I think it's better to make such stress tests randomized instead. Such
regularity might hit even other unintended effects.

Signals: requested changes
Last comment 2026-11-20 [PATCH] mm/memory: don't map hardware-poisoned pages in finish_fault() Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch prevents hardware-poisoned pages from being mapped in the page cache when a large file folio cannot be split by memory_failure(). Instead, it maps each poisoned page individually, allowing it to take its own fault and resulting in a SIGBUS signal. This change addresses an issue where a process touching a poisoned page would instead receive a machine check. The patch builds upon existing functionality in finish_fault() and is designed to prevent the mapping of hardware-poisoned pages when using large file folios.

5 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Zi Yan ↳ replying to Kiryl (Meta) Reviewed-by Positive LLM

Show original comment

Yan, Zi

  • [1/3] mm: page_alloc: add a watermark check that knows the migratetype
  • [2/3] mm: page_alloc: make the high-order watermark check honour ALLOC_NOFRAGMENT
  • [3/3] mm: vmscan: tell direct reclaim the allocation is ALLOC_NOFRAGMENT
Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch series addresses an issue where the high-order watermark check in the Linux kernel incorrectly counts free pages of every migratetype, leading to stalls and inefficiencies under memory pressure. The patches modify the watermark checks to honor the ALLOC_NOFRAGMENT flag, which is set for slowpath allocations below pageblock_order. This change improves performance by preventing unnecessary compactions and direct reclaims, resulting in a significant reduction in 64K mTHP faults and improved overall system efficiency.

Discussions / RFCs (0)
No activity
Reviews Given (15)
Last comment 2026-11-20 Re: [PATCH v2 00/39] arm64: Add support for FEAT_NMI Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series adds support for FEAT_NMI (NMI) feature on arm64 architecture. The problem it solves is a race condition where a CPU entering power-down idle state masks SDEI, causing other CPUs to hang in csd_lock_wait() when trying to send an interrupt. The approach taken is to either fix the firmware or modify the kernel to never signal a CPU with SDEI masked, by adding a handshake between the signalling CPU and the cpu_pm notifier.

2 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Kiryl (Meta) (author) Neutral LLM
The author acknowledged that the sdei_event_signal() function returns -EAGAIN when a masked target is signaled, and explained how it handles this situation by sending an IPI to the CPU instead of SDEI. The author also provided patches to modify the arm64_backtrace_ipi() function to use the new sdei_block_signal() function.
Show original comment 
Here is what I meant.

sdei_event_signal() refuses a masked target with -EAGAIN, and the
backtrace sends those CPUs the regular IPI. A PE that sees a signal on
its way stays out of the power-down state; the mask paths that cannot
fail wait for it, up to a millisecond.

On QEMU with TF-A minus the v2.13 check, masking a CPU through
cpu_pm_enter() and then signalling it gets -EAGAIN instead of the wedge,
and sysrq-l still goes over SDEI to the other CPUs.

I can send proper patches if it looks reasonable.

+/*
+ * Event 0 must not land on a masked PE. The sender and the masking PE
+ * each set their own flag, then read the other's.
+ */
+static DEFINE_PER_CPU(bool, sdei_pe_unmasked);
+static DEFINE_PER_CPU(bool, sdei_signal_pending);
+
+static bool sdei_mark_masked(void)
+{
+	this_cpu_write(sdei_pe_unmasked, false);
+	smp_mb();
+	return this_cpu_read(sdei_signal_pending);
+}
+
+static void sdei_block_signal(void)
+{
+	int i;
+
+	if (!sdei_mark_masked())
+		return;
+
+	for (i = 0; i < USEC_PER_MSEC && this_cpu_read(sdei_signal_pending); i++)
+		udelay(1);
+}
+
 int sdei_mask_local_cpu(void)
 {
 	int err;
 
+	sdei_block_signal();
+
 	err = invoke_sdei_fn(SDEI_1_0_FN_SDEI_PE_MASK, 0, 0, 0, 0, 0, NULL);
 	if (err && err != -EIO) {
 		pr_warn_once("failed to mask CPU[%u]: %d\n",
@@ -330,6 +360,7 @@ int sdei_unmask_local_cpu(void)
 		return err;
 	}
 
+	this_cpu_write(sdei_pe_unmasked, true);
 	return 0;
 }

Reviewer Jinjie Ruan noted that when a CPU is in power-down idle state and SDEI is masked, the firmware may not properly handle event 0, leading to a situation where the SGI stays active and other CPUs hang in csd_lock_wait(). The reviewer suggested two checks to confirm if this issue exists in the firmware and proposed three possible solutions: fixing the firmware, modifying the kernel to never signal a CPU with masked SDEI, or sending the IPI first and then SDEI. This patch series addresses issues in the Linux kernel's swap cache writeout and cluster table management. It allows swapcache writeout without anonymous mappings, ensures that cluster tables are published after reacquiring allocator locks, and prevents relocation of tableless clusters. From: Nhat Pham <nphamcs@gmail.com>
Subject: mm: vmscan: allow writeout of swapcache folios without anon mappings
Date: Tue, 10 Nov 2026 07:14:05 -0800

Patch series "mm: fix swapcache writeout and cluster table races", v2.

Reviews of vswap and v1 of this series found bugs in existing swap code. 
Allow swapcache writeout without anon mappings, publish cluster tables
with the allocator locks held, and leave tableless clusters to the
allocator.


This patch (of 3):

Unfaulted zswap readahead folios can be dirty and in swapcache without
an anonymous mapping. pageout() rejects them before swap_writeout(),
so reclaim cannot write them out while their swap entries remain
referenced.

Allow swapcache folios through the filesystem folio check. They already
have swap entries and can use the existing swap writeout path. Anonymous
folios outside swapcache have no mapping and already return PAGE_KEEP.

From: Nhat Pham <nphamcs@gmail.com>
Subject: mm/swap: skip relocation of tableless clusters
Date: Tue, 10 Nov 2026 07:14:07 -0800

A stale reclaimer can reach relocate_cluster() after another allocator
re-isolates an emptied cluster and drops its locks to populate the table.
The cluster has zero count, CLUSTER_FLAG_NONE and no table, so relocation
can free it again and dereference NULL in the DEBUG_VM empty check.

    CPU0                                    CPU1
    Reclaimer                               Allocator
    alloc_swap_scan_cluster()
      cluster_reclaim_range()
        spin_unlock(&ci->lock)
        __try_to_reclaim_swap()
          swap_cache_del_folio()
            __swap_cache_del_folio()
              __swap_cache_do_del_folio()
                __swap_cluster_free_entries()
                  free_cluster()
                    __free_cluster()
                      swap_cluster_free_table()
            swap_cluster_unlock(ci)
                                            isolate_lock_cluster()
                                              swap_cluster_populate()
                                                drop ci and outer locks
                                                swap_cluster_alloc_table()
        spin_lock(&ci->lock)
      relocate_cluster()
        free_cluster()
          __free_cluster()
            swap_cluster_assert_empty()
              __swap_table_get()  /* NULL table */

Leave tableless clusters to the allocator under ci->lock. This also stops
a stale reclaimer from returning a cluster to the free list while table
population is pending, avoiding the resulting FREE-to-NONE state cycle.
The existing cluster lock serializes the table check and relocation with
population.

Link: https://patch.msgid.link/20261110151407.789960-4-nphamcs@gmail.com
Fixes: 07adc4cf1ecd ("mm, swap: implement dynamic allocation of swap table")

 		return;

 		if (ci->flags != CLUSTER_FLAG_FREE)

These are the data summaries/details with v4's zswap store batching
only (patches 6-7), and with zswap compress batching added in patch 8,
to help us better understand the wins.

Batch compression of PMD THP with ZSTD yields an additional 6.56% Sys
time savings and additional 1.12% Throughput improvement over zswap
store batching.
As expected, PMD THP demonstrates the most gains overall with
store+compress batching.

As mentioned in the cover letter, as other kernel components related
to mTHP become more efficient, the net realized performance benefit
can only be better with patch 8 than without.

[PATCH] mm/gup: unshare-check every page of a pte batch Neutral LLM

This patch addresses a bug in the gup (get user pages) helper, where it incorrectly assumes that all pages in a batch are writable or anonymous exclusive. The fix involves checking each page individually for suitability, ensuring that only writable batches and GUP-fast are unchanged.

2 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants

David Hildenbrand (Arm) Positive LLM

[PATCH v5 0/8] mm/gup: walk and batch whole PTE tables in slow GUP 8 patches
Show 8 individual patches
  • [1/8] mm/gup: break out gup_fill_pages() helper
  • [2/8] mm/gup: break out gup_flush_pages() helper
  • [3/8] mm/gup: convert follow_page_mask() to return a long
  • [4/8] mm/gup: split out folio grab-and-store helper
  • [5/8] mm/gup: break out gup_lookup_pte() helper
  • [6/8] mm/gup: return a huge page's full count from follow_page_mask()
  • [7/8] mm/gup: walk multiple PTEs per follow_page_pte() call
  • [8/8] mm/gup: batch contiguous same-folio PTEs into one refcount grab
Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch series improves performance in slow GUP (get user pages) by batching whole PTE tables and reducing the number of refcount grabs. The current slow path resolves and commits one PTE per call, leading to a restart of the page table descent and multiple refcount grabs for each page. This series brings the slow path up to speed with the fast path by introducing batch-length helpers and splitting out various functions to improve performance. The patches are reviewed in steps, starting with no-functional-change refactors and ending with the multi-PTE walk and same-folio batch commit. Measurements show a significant improvement in get time for large folios, with median times reduced by up to 12.8x.

Shakeel Butt

17 items
Patches Submitted (10)
[PATCH] cgroup: allocate the inode for cgroup_may_write() before cgroup_mutex Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch addresses a performance issue in the Linux kernel's cgroup subsystem, specifically related to inode allocation and locking. The problem arises when the memcg OOM killer runs from cgroup_may_write() while holding cgroup_mutex and cgroup_threadgroup_rwsem, causing significant latency due to inode reclamation. To resolve this, the patch allocates the inode before taking cgroup_mutex and adds it under kernfs_rwsem using kernfs_get_inode_locked(). This change improves performance by reducing the time spent in memcg reclaim with cgroup_mutex held.

Last comment 2026-11-21 [PATCH] ipmi:msghandler: Free queued requests of a destroyed user Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch addresses a memory leak issue in the IPMI (Intelligent Platform Management Interface) driver, where requests from destroyed users are not properly freed when the BMC (Baseboard Management Controller) stops responding to IPMI requests. The patch introduces a new function to free queued requests of a destroyed user, preventing the accumulation of closed users and their messages, which can consume significant memory over time. This issue was identified in Meta's fleet, where hosts with malfunctioning BMCs accumulated millions of closed users, leading to memory usage issues. The patch has been tested on QEMU with a fake BMC that stops answering IPMI requests and shows significant reductions in the number of held closed users.

Last comment 2026-11-19 [PATCH] kernfs: allocate in open before taking the active reference Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch improves the performance of kernfs by allocating memory for open files and their associated seq_file structures before taking an active reference to the file. This change reduces the time spent waiting for reclaim operations, particularly in scenarios where cgroup operations are performed concurrently. The patch also ensures that if a file is removed while being opened, the operation fails with -ENODEV as expected.

Ongoing Submitted 2026-11-11 Last comment 2026-11-17 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH v4] kernfs: allocate inodes and dentry outside kernfs_rwsem Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch addresses long stalls in kernfs due to holding the kernfs_rwsem lock while allocating inodes and dentries. It achieves this by allocating inodes before taking the lock, using kernfs_get_inode_locked(), and only holding the lock around inode lookups. This approach reduces contention on the lock and prevents stalls caused by memory reclaim. The patch also fixes a related issue with kernfs_get_parent_dentry() by holding the lock only during inode lookup. Testing shows significant reductions in wait times for various tasks.

[PATCH 0/6] kernfs: stop taking kernfs_iattr_rwsem for writing in mkdir/rmdir 6 patches
Show 6 individual patches
  • [1/6] kernfs: report times and link count from the node in getattr
  • [2/6] kernfs: don't copy times and link count into the inode on refresh
  • [3/6] kernfs: don't take kernfs_iattr_rwsem for the directory counters
  • [4/6] kernfs: don't take kernfs_iattr_rwsem to publish KERNFS_REMOVING
  • [5/6] kernfs: update a directory's times without kernfs_iattr_rwsem
  • [6/6] kernfs: don't take kernfs_iattr_rwsem in __kernfs_remove()
Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch series addresses a performance issue in the Linux kernel's kernfs subsystem by reducing unnecessary locking during mkdir and rmdir operations. The current implementation holds multiple locks, including kernfs_iattr_rwsem, which can lead to stalls on large systems under memory pressure. The patches remove the write lock from kernfs_iattr_rwsem for these operations, instead relying on other mechanisms such as seqlocks and inode setup to maintain consistency. This change reduces latency and improves performance in various scenarios, including concurrent mkdir/rmdir and stat() operations.

[RFC PATCH 0/7] memcg: effective high limit that avoids isolation issues 7 patches
Show 7 individual patches
  • [1/7] memcg: add helpers to handle memory.high overage in long operations
  • [2/7] mm/gup: handle memory.high overage while populating
  • [3/7] mm, fs: handle memory.high overage in buffered reads and writes
  • [4/7] mm/shmem: handle memory.high overage in fallocate and reads
  • [5/7] mm/khugepaged: handle memory.high overage in MADV_COLLAPSE
  • [6/7] memcg: remove synchronous memory.high enforcement
  • [7/7] selftests/cgroup: make test_memcg_high_sync check that throttling holds
Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch series aims to address isolation issues caused by memcg limits, which can lead to tasks being stuck in limit enforcement and holding locks, causing other tasks to wait. The current approach of enforcing memory.high limits in the charge path is problematic as it causes priority inversion. This series proposes an alternative solution that avoids this issue by introducing an 'effective high limit' that takes into account the actual available resources, rather than just the memcg limit. This should reduce the likelihood of tasks being stuck in limit enforcement and holding locks, thereby improving system responsiveness.

Ongoing Submitted 2026-11-12 🔥 TODAY [PATCH] memcg: flush nr_charged_bytes when the obj stock trylock fails Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch fixes a memory accounting issue where the nr_charged_bytes counter in an object cgroup (objcg) can grow indefinitely when trylocking fails due to concurrent access from interrupt code, such as RCU callbacks. This causes inaccurate memory usage reports and can lead to incorrect reclaim or OOM killing of cgroups. The fix involves uncharging the whole pages in nr_charged_bytes whenever the trylock fails.

------------------------------------------------------
From: Shakeel Butt <shakeel.butt@linux.dev>
Subject: memcg: flush nr_charged_bytes when the obj stock trylock fails
Date: Wed, 11 Nov 2026 17:32:20 -0800

When interrupt code, such as an RCU callback in softirq, runs on top of a
task that holds the obj stock lock, its trylock fails and the bytes go to
objcg->nr_charged_bytes.  Nothing empties that counter while every CPU
using the objcg keeps it in its stock, so it can grow without limit.

Users see memory.current and the "kernel" field of memory.stat keep
growing while the cgroup's real usage does not.  With memory.high or
memory.max set, the cgroup is reclaimed, throttled or even OOM-killed for
memory it does not use.  The counter is 32-bit, so after 4 GiB it wraps,
and those 4 GiB stay charged to the cgroup and its ancestors until reboot,
even after the cgroup is removed.

Fix it by uncharging the whole pages in nr_charged_bytes whenever the
trylock fails.

Link: https://patch.msgid.link/20261112013220.4064478-1-shakeel.butt@linux.dev
Fixes: 200577f69f29 ("memcg: objcg stock trylock without irq disabling")

Ongoing Submitted 2026-11-12 🔥 TODAY [PATCH] mm: list_lru: don't use a new memcg's lists for an offline memcg Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch fixes a bug in the Linux kernel's memory cgroup (memcg) implementation where an offline memcg's lists are reused by a new memcg, leading to list corruption and crashes when THPs are freed. The fix involves recording the owning memcg in each list_lru_memcg and treating the index as empty if its lists belong to another memcg. This prevents concurrent changes to the parent's list from leaving pointers to freed folios.

0 participants
Andrew Morton ↳ replying to Shakeel Butt Reviewed-by Neutral LLM
Andrew Morton noted that the patch was merged into the mm-hotfixes-stable branch and therefore dropped from the -mm tree.
Show original comment 
The quilt patch titled
     Subject: mm: list_lru: don't use a new memcg's lists for an offline memcg
has been removed from the -mm tree.  Its filename was
     mm-list_lru-dont-use-a-new-memcgs-lists-for-an-offline-memcg.patch

This patch was dropped because it was merged into the mm-hotfixes-stable branch
of git://git.kernel.org/pub/scm/linux/kernel/git/akpm/mm

------------------------------------------------------
From: Shakeel Butt <shakeel.butt@linux.dev>
Subject: mm: list_lru: don't use a new memcg's lists for an offline memcg
Date: Thu, 12 Nov 2026 14:12:48 -0800

A memcg's list_lru lists are found by its kmemcg_id, which is its
private ID. Offlining a memcg moves its lists to the parent and frees
the ID right away, with no RCU grace period. The next new memcg can get
the same ID and allocate its own lists at that index.

A caller that holds only the RCU read lock, such as
__folio_unqueue_deferred_split(), can still hold the offline memcg. If
it is preempted until the ID is reused, it then locks the new memcg's
list and unlinks a folio that now sits on the parent's list, without
holding the parent's lock. A concurrent change to the parent's list can
then leave a pointer to a freed folio on it.

Users see list corruption reports, crashes or silent memory corruption
when memory cgroups are removed and created while THPs are freed, on
kernels with preemptible RCU, which is the default on x86 and arm64.
They can also see a warning from memcg_reparent_list_lru_one() when the
new memcg is removed, because the wrong unlink made its count negative.

Fix it by recording the owning memcg in each list_lru_memcg, and by
treating the index as empty when its lists belong to another memcg. The
lookup then moves up to the parent, whose list holds the folio.

[akpm@linux-foundation.org: verify memcg owner before skipping allocation]
  Link: https://lore.kernel.org/20261112161502.18d9d72c0139b8f69bb88ff4@linux-foundation.org
Link: https://patch.msgid.link/20261112221248.3512447-1-shakeel.butt@linux.dev
Fixes: 28e98022b31e ("mm/list_lru: simplify reparenting and initial allocation")

Ongoing Submitted 2026-11-12 🔥 TODAY [PATCH v2] mm: list_lru: fix NULL deref when a folio moves to the root memcg Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch fixes a NULL pointer dereference bug that occurs when a folio moves to the root memory cgroup between two lookups. The bug is caused by the slow path of the `folio_memcg_list_lru_alloc` function not checking if the root memcg has its own lists before allocating them. The fix involves adding an additional check for the root memcg using the `memcg_list_lru_allocated` function, similar to the fast path. This prevents a NULL pointer dereference when the folio is moved up to the root between the two lookups.

------------------------------------------------------
From: Shakeel Butt <shakeel.butt@linux.dev>
Subject: mm: list_lru: fix NULL deref when a folio moves to the root memcg
Date: Wed, 11 Nov 2026 22:12:31 -0800

folio_memcg_list_lru_alloc() makes sure that a folio's memcg has its own
lists in a list_lru, such as the THP deferred split queue.  It looks up
the folio's memcg twice: a fast path under RCU checks if the lists exist,
and a slow path takes a reference and allocates them.  The fast path
returns early for the root memcg, which uses the global lists.  The slow
path does not check for the root.

The memcg can change between the two lookups: when a cgroup is removed,
its folios are moved to the parent memcg.  If the folio is moved up to the
root in between, the slow path gets the root, and __memcg_list_lru_alloc()
dereferences the root's parent, which is NULL.

Users see a NULL pointer dereference oops while a THP is allocated, such
as in a page fault, in khugepaged or at swap-in.  tmpfs huge pages are
affected too, and for them the oops can also happen in reclaim, for
example in kswapd.  The task that hits the oops is killed, or the machine
panics if panic_on_oops is set.

The race is narrow: the folio has to be moved up to the root right between
the two lookups.  For example, its cgroup is directly below the root and
is removed, or has its memory controller disabled, at that moment.

Fix it by checking memcg_list_lru_allocated() again in the slow path, like
the fast path does.  It returns true for the root, which needs no lists of
its own.

Link: https://patch.msgid.link/20261112061231.2469026-1-shakeel.butt@linux.dev
Fixes: ae64f07a6a40 ("mm: list_lru: introduce folio_memcg_list_lru_alloc()")
Signed-off-by: Shakeel Butt <shakeel.butt@linux.dev>
Signed-off-by: Andrew Morton <akpm@linux-foundation.org>
Reported-by: Chris Mason <mason@kernel.org>
Acked-by: Muchun Song (ByteDance) <muchun.song@linux.dev>
Reviewed-by: Johannes Weiner <hannes@cmpxchg.org>
Reviewed-by: Harry Yoo (Meta) <harry@kernel.org>

Cc: David Hildenbrand <david@kernel.org>
Cc: Michal Hocko <mhocko@kernel.org>

Cc: Qi Zheng <qi.zheng@linux.dev>

+	 */
 	memcg = get_mem_cgroup_from_folio(folio);
-	res = __memcg_list_lru_alloc(memcg, lru, gfp);
+	if (!memcg_list_lru_allocated(memcg, lru))
+		res = __memcg_list_lru_alloc(memcg, lru, gfp);
 	mem_cgroup_put(memcg);
 	return res;
 }
_

Ongoing Submitted 2026-11-11 Last comment 2026-11-16 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH] mm: mglru: splice all generations when reparenting a memcg Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch fixes a list_del corruption issue in the Linux kernel's memory management system (mm) by splicing all generations of MGLRU lists when reparenting a memcg (memory control group). The problem occurs when a memcg goes offline and its MGLRU lists are moved to the parent, but only certain generations are moved. This can lead to a folio being left on a freed memcg, causing a use-after-free error. To fix this, the patch moves all MAX_NR_GENS lists, not just those from min_seq to max_seq, ensuring that all folios are properly reparented and avoiding the corruption issue.

Ongoing Submitted 2026-11-11 Last comment 2026-11-20 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH 0/2] crypto: use vmalloc() for large stream workspaces 2 patches
Show 2 individual patches
  • [1/2] crypto: zstd - use vmalloc() for stream workspaces Neutral Submitted 2026-11-11Last comment 2026-11-13 Rik van Riel Johannes Weiner Chris Down
  • [2/2] crypto: deflate - use vmalloc() for stream workspaces Neutral Submitted 2026-11-11Last comment 2026-11-13 Rik van Riel Johannes Weiner Chris Down
Neutral LLM

The patch series aims to optimize memory allocation for large stream workspaces in the crypto subsystem by switching from kmalloc() to vmalloc(). This change is motivated by the observation that kvmalloc() can lead to inefficient high-order allocations, resulting in significant memory usage. The patches replace kmalloc() with vmalloc() for zstd and deflate algorithms, reducing workspace backing size and improving performance.

4 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Rik van Riel Reviewed-by Positive LLM
Identified the issue of high-order allocations due to kvmalloc() and suggested using vmalloc() instead. Provided Reviewed-by tags for both patches.
Signals: NEEDS_WORK
Johannes Weiner Reviewed-by Positive LLM
Agreed with Rik's assessment and provided additional Reviewed-by tags for both patches.
Show original comment 
On Wed, Nov 11, 2026 at 07:07:39AM -0800, Usama Arif wrote:
> The zstd acomp streams retain their workspaces after the last transform
> has been freed. The first is allocated during transform initialization;
> additional per-CPU streams are allocated on demand. They are released
> when the algorithm is unregistered.
> 
> In a 4 KiB-page x86-64 build, each stream requests 1,566,281 bytes.
> If kvmalloc() succeeds via kmalloc(), it occupies an order-9 block
> (2 MiB). vmalloc() needs 383 backing pages (1,532 KiB), 516 KiB fewer
> per stream. Across 384 populated streams, that is 193.5 MiB less
> workspace backing if all would have used kmalloc(). There is no such
> gain when kvmalloc() already falls back to vmalloc().
> 
> Use vmalloc() and vfree() directly to avoid the high-order allocation.
> This uses vmalloc address space, page tables and bookkeeping; the
> figures above compare workspace backing only.
> 
> Signed-off-by: Usama Arif <usama.arif@linux.dev>

Nice catch. Given the lifetime of these streams, there really isn't
enough turnover to justify the kmalloc optimization to begin with.

Reviewed-by: Johannes Weiner <hannes@cmpxchg.org>
Chris Down Reviewed-by Tested-by Neutral LLM
Pointed out that vmalloc() doesn't avoid high-order allocations and suggested rephrasing the patch description. Provided Reviewed-by and Tested-by tags for both patches.
Show original comment 
Hi Usama,

Usama Arif writes:
>Use vmalloc() and vfree() directly to avoid the high-order allocation.

vmalloc() doesn't avoid high-order allocations any more. Since
a06157804399 ("mm/vmalloc: request large order pages from buddy
allocator"), vm_area_alloc_pages() first tries the largest order that
fits, and splits the block up afterwards. So for these 383 pages it still
asks for an order-8 block first, then order 6, 5, 4, 3, 2 and 1.

What the patch does avoid is kmalloc() rounding the 1.5 MiB up to a 2 MiB
block, which is where the 516 KiB per stream comes from. Maybe say that
instead?

Once that's fixed, please feel free to add:

Reviewed-by: Chris Down <chris@chrisdown.name>
Tested-by: Chris Down <chris@chrisdown.name>

Chris

Last comment 2026-11-16 [PATCH v2 0/4] mm: fixes for THP migration, swap-in and device-private smaps 4 patches
Show 4 individual patches
  • [1/4] mm/migrate_device: avoid leaking folio references during THP splitting Neutral Last comment 2026-11-16 David Hildenbrand (Arm) Zi Yan
  • [2/4] mm: don't PTE-batch a swap-in of a folio with a hardware-poisoned page Neutral Last comment 2026-11-16 Joshua Hahn David Hildenbrand (Arm) Zi Yan
  • [3/4] fs/proc/task_mmu: don't read young/dirty from a non-present PMD in smaps
  • [4/4] fs/proc/task_mmu: account device-private PMDs as present in smaps
Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series addresses four issues related to Transparent Huge Pages (THP) migration, PTE swap-in, and device-private smaps in the Linux kernel. The first fix prevents a folio reference leak during THP splitting. The second patch avoids mapping hardware-poisoned pages of large anonymous swap-cache folios during PTE swap-in batching. The third change stops reading accessed and dirty bits from non-present PMDs in smaps, where they represent softleaf entries rather than actual state. Finally, the fourth patch accounts for device-private PMDs using mapcounts in smaps, ensuring shared mappings receive proportional Page-Sharing (PSS) and exclusive mappings are counted as private.

[PATCH 0/3] perf/core: Fix software event sample data, then stop zeroing it 3 patches
Show 3 individual patches
  • [1/3] perf/core: Fix perf_sample_data reuse across software events
  • [2/3] perf/core: Don't let BPF programs read an uninitialized sample address
  • [3/3] perf/core: Don't zero perf_sample_data in do_perf_sw_event()
Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch series fixes two bugs in the Linux kernel's perf/core module related to software event sample data. The first bug occurs when a sampling event inherits an uninitialized struct perf_sample_data, leading to incorrect data and potential crashes. The second bug allows BPF programs to read uninitialized stack contents through the bpf_perf_event_data::addr field. The patches fix these issues by initializing the struct for each matching event, preventing BPF programs from reading uninitialized data, and removing unnecessary zero-initialization of the struct in do_perf_sw_event(). These changes improve performance and prevent potential security vulnerabilities.

Ongoing Submitted 2026-11-13 Last comment 2026-11-16 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH] Revert "sched/fair: Add SIS_UTIL support to select_idle_capacity()" Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch reverts a previous change that added SIS_UTIL support to the select_idle_capacity() function in the Linux kernel's scheduler. The reverted change caused issues when the scan budget was zero, leading to incorrect idle CPU selection and increased latency for certain workloads. The patch restores the original behavior of searching the allowed domain for an idle CPU, which can increase scan CPU cost but ensures accurate idle CPU selection. This change is intended to fix a performance regression introduced by the previous commit.

Rik Riel ↳ replying to Usama Arif Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Rik Riel questioned whether the asymmetrical selection should prioritize any CPU 'close enough' to the required capacity (X) when dealing with SMT and power-driven hardware scaling, suggesting a threshold of 3/4 of X as a compromise between preventing overloading small cores and allowing tasks to run immediately.
Show original comment 
Afaik, the asymmetrical selection is supposed
to solve a very specific problem: when a task
requires X amount of CPU capacity to run, we
try to avoid scheduling it on a CPU that has
only a fraction of X available.

This creates the issue that a task that appears
to "need" exactly X, because it's been running
on a CPU with full available capacity, ends up
not being scheduled at all when the available
idle core has X-3% available capacity, because
that CPU happens to spend 3% of its time handling
interrupts, or running something else.

In that situation, it will be better to run the
task on the CPU with 97% capacity right now,
than to not run the task until a CPU frees up
later.

Things like SMT and power-driven hardware
scaling complicate the matter further, because
we simply cannot see the full amount of capacity 
that is available on each CPU.

That makes me wonder if we should simply accept
any CPU that's "close enough to X" when dealing
with asymmetrical placement.

Say, anything that's above 3/4 of the capacity
of X would be considered good enough to run the task.

That still prevents us from placing a CPU intensive
task on a little core in a big.LITTLE system, or
from placing a task on a CPU that spends 80% of its
time handling interrupts, but it will allow a task
to run right now, when the capacity imbalance is small.

Does that make sense, or am I forgetting something
important?

-- 
All Rights Reversed.

Reviewer Vincent Guittot noted that the current SIS_UTIL algorithm is too cautious, particularly for systems with a small number of CPUs, and suggested considering setting a shorter slice for latency-sensitive tasks to run first even on busy CPUs.

We want to keep same behavior for select_idle_cpu and
select_idle_capacity with both using the same SIS_UTIL algorithm.

That being said, the current algorithm is probably a bit too cautious,
particularly for systems with a small number of CPUs

And for a latency-sensitive workload, it would be good to consider
setting a shorter slice to the latency sensistive task would run 1st
even on a busy CPU
View on lore ↗
Signals: algorithm needs adjustment, considering alternative approach
Andrea Righi ↳ replying to Vincent Guittot Inline Review Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Andrea Righi noted that the current implementation of select_idle_capacity() returns before examining any candidates in the scan domain when the scan budget is zero, and suggested relaxing the capacity-fit rule inside the earlier checks in select_idle_sibling() to avoid reaching the zero-budget bailout.
Show original comment 
Right, and when the scan budget is exhausted select_idle_capacity()
returns the best idle candidate found so far, even if it doesn't fit.

However, in the zero-budget case, we return before examining any
candidates in the scan domain. Relaxing the capacity-fit rule inside
that scan wouldn't change the outcome, because we never reach that
check.

Maybe Rik's suggestion could instead help with the earlier checks in
select_idle_sibling()? Accepting an idle target, previous or recently
used CPU whose capacity is "close enough" could avoid reaching the
zero-budget bailout.

The tradeoff is that we'd accept a potentially weaker CPU immediately,
without searching for a better one.

Thanks,
-Andrea
View on lore ↗

+static int asym_idle_retry(struct task_struct *p, int prev, int target,
+			   int recent)
+{
+	if (choose_idle_cpu(target, p))
+		return target;
+

+	    choose_idle_cpu(prev, p))
+		return prev;

+	if (recent != prev && recent != target &&
+	    cpus_share_cache(recent, target) &&
+	    choose_idle_cpu(recent, p))

+	return target;

Last comment 2026-11-19 [PATCH 0/2] x86/kstack_erase: Faster stack poisoning, plus a startup build fix 2 patches
Show 2 individual patches
  • [1/2] x86/boot/startup: Disable kernel stack depth tracking Neutral Last comment 2026-11-19 Nick Desaulniers
  • [2/2] x86/kstack_erase: Use REP STOSQ for large stack fills Neutral Last comment 2026-11-19 Nick Desaulniers
Neutral LLM

The patch series aims to optimize the kernel stack poisoning mechanism, specifically for x86-64 architectures. It introduces a new approach using REP STOSQ instructions for large stack fills, which significantly reduces the overhead of stack poisoning. Additionally, it includes an independent build fix to address issues with Clang and AMD memory encryption.

2 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Nick Desaulniers ↳ replying to Usama Arif Reviewed-by Neutral LLM
Raised a concern about the use of REP STOSQ instructions being limited to this patch series and suggested checking for other instances in the kernel tree. Provided an example from memset64.
Show original comment 
On Thu, Nov 19, 2026 at 1:32 PM Usama Arif <usama.arif@linux.dev> wrote:
>
> With KSTACK_ERASE, every return to user space poisons the part of the
> task stack that was used since the previous return. The generic
> __stackleak_poison() does that one word per loop iteration, so a syscall
> that went 1-2 KiB deep pays for 128-256 separate stores. On Meta hosts
> running many small VMs, stackleak_erase() accounts for about 1.5% of
> non-idle host kernel cycles.
>
> Override __stackleak_poison() on x86-64, as s390 already does, through
> the hook added by commit 491a78663e03 ("stackleak: allow to specify arch
> specific stackleak poison function"). Fill spans larger than 512 bytes
> with REP STOSQ and keep the word loop for shorter ones, where the fixed
> startup cost of REP STOSQ does not pay off. REP STOSQ starts to win at
> about 300-400 bytes on Zen 5c and Cooper Lake, so on both no span gets
> slower.
>
> The fill is inline asm, so the noinstr function still makes no calls.
> memset64() would be the same instruction, but it is only static inline,
> and with KMSAN it is an out-of-line function.
>
> In a Zen 5c KVM guest, stackleak_erase() got about 35% cheaper per return
> to user space (71 ns to 46 ns, scan included) over a futex, epoll, procfs
> and Unix socket syscall mix. Same-vCPU futex wake/wait and Unix socket
> sendmsg()/recvmsg() ping-pong round trips got 5-8% faster.
>
> Signed-off-by: Usama Arif <usama.arif@linux.dev>
> ---
>  arch/x86/include/asm/processor.h | 25 +++++++++++++++++++++++++
>  1 file changed, 25 insertions(+)
>
> diff --git a/arch/x86/include/asm/processor.h b/arch/x86/include/asm/processor.h
> index 77488ff5eb8c7c..049231d484ad0e 100644
> --- a/arch/x86/include/asm/processor.h
> +++ b/arch/x86/include/asm/processor.h
> @@ -580,6 +580,31 @@ static __always_inline bool on_thread_stack(void)
>                                current_stack_pointer) < THREAD_SIZE;
>  }
>
> +#ifdef CONFIG_X86_64
> +#define __stackleak_poison __stackleak_poison
> +static __always_inline void __stackleak_poison(unsigned long erase_low,
> +                                              unsigned long erase_high,
> +                                              unsigned long poison)
> +{
> +       unsigned long count;
> +
> +       /* Short spans do not amortize the startup cost of REP STOSQ. */
> +       if (erase_high <= erase_low + 512) {
> +               while (erase_low < erase_high) {
> +                       *(unsigned long *)erase_low = poison;
> +                       erase_low += sizeof(unsigned long);
> +               }
> +               return;
> +       }
> +
> +       count = (erase_high - erase_low) / sizeof(unsigned long);
> +       asm volatile("rep stosq"
> +                    : "+D" (erase_low), "+c" (count)
> +                    : "a" (poison)
> +                    : "memory");

Surely this isn't the only place using `rep stosq` in the tree, right?
It's not, see memset64 in arch/x86/include/asm/string_64.h.

> +}
> +#endif
> +
>  #ifdef CONFIG_PARAVIRT_XXL
>  #include <asm/paravirt.h>
>  #else

> 2.53.1-Meta

~Nick Desaulniers

On Thu, Nov 19, 2026 at 1:31 PM Usama Arif <usama.arif@linux.dev> wrote:
>
> Commit 57fbad15c2ee ("stackleak: Rename STACKLEAK to KSTACK_ERASE")
> renamed DISABLE_STACKLEAK_PLUGIN to DISABLE_KSTACK_ERASE everywhere
> except in arch/x86/boot/startup/Makefile. The old name now expands to
> nothing, so the startup code is built with stack depth tracking enabled.
>
> The startup code is linked with every symbol prefixed by __pi_, so that
> it cannot call into the rest of the kernel by accident. It has no alias
> for the tracking callback, and tracking stack depth there is of no use.
>
> At the default KSTACK_ERASE_TRACK_MIN_SIZE of 100 no startup function is
> large enough to be tracked, so this went unnoticed. With Clang,
> CONFIG_AMD_MEM_ENCRYPT=y and a minimum below about 90 bytes,
> pvalidate_4k_page(), snp_cpuid() and do_vc_no_ghcb() get a call to
> __sanitizer_cov_stack_depth() and vmlinux fails to link:
>
>   ld.lld: error: undefined symbol: __pi___sanitizer_cov_stack_depth
>
> Use DISABLE_KSTACK_ERASE, which disables tracking with either compiler.
>
> Fixes: 57fbad15c2ee ("stackleak: Rename STACKLEAK to KSTACK_ERASE")

tsk tsk, Kees your `grep` might be borked!

Nico Pache ↳ replying to Usama Arif Needs Work LLM
Expressed concerns about Usama's patch, citing potential performance issues with KSM and suggesting an alternative approach that addresses the underlying issue in a cleaner manner.

On 11/18/26 2:27 PM, Usama Arif wrote:

> 
> Hey Nico,

Hey Usama!

> 
> I think I have had this conversation either on the previous revision

> again.
> 
> Isnt above a good thing? KSM is a memory saving feature, and this
> is resulting in 1G being freed instantly?

Not necessarily, for two reason (a), we are ignoring the users defined 
pages_to_scan, and (b), this leads to KSM using way more CPU time than 
the user expected, and in our customers cases, a overall system 
regression (outside of KSM also losing some effectiveness).>

> As an alternate to having this patch, should we just have patch 1

Me and David have been discussing the opposite. I was going to drop 
patch 1 as patch 2 solves the underlying issue in a fairly clean manner.

> 
> If you have a large number of THPs on your machine, using KSM is a very
> bad idea IMO due to all the splits that happen.
> IMO, there are 2 optimum host conditions:
> - A large number of THPs, and KSM disabled, hence shrinker can stay on.
> - Very low number of THPs, KSM can be enabled or disabled, but as there
> are a low number of THPs, the shrinker can be turned off.

This requires the user to be aware of these limitations and properly 
configure this system. With this patch we remove the weird interaction 
without needing any config changes. I think in our previous discussion 
David pushed back saying that it is not uncommon to see MADV_MERGEABLE 
and MADV_HUGEPAGE.

I think the approach in patch #2 is the most sound approach.

Let me know if that clears everything up!

Last comment 2026-11-20 Re: [PATCH 2/2] x86/kstack_erase: Use REP STOSQ for large stack fills Neutral LLM

This patch modifies the x86 kernel's stack erasure code to use REP STOSQ for large stack fills, addressing an issue where rounding up or down the erase range could write to live stack or STACK_END_MAGIC.

1 reviewer comment — 0 participants
0 participants
David Laight ↳ replying to Usama Arif Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer pointed out that rounding up the range in kstack_erase would write live stack, while rounding down could write STACK_END_MAGIC when the stack was used to the end, and requested a solution for handling this edge case. Reviewer David Laight questioned the effectiveness of the code, pointing out that it stops scanning after finding 128 bytes of KSTACK_ERASE_POISON, which could lead to incorrect results in cases where a function has a large on-stack buffer. He suggested rounding down the length and also asked if STACK_END_MAGIC and STACK_ERASE_POISON need different values.
Show original comment 
On Fri, 20 Nov 2026 18:46:01 +0000
Usama Arif <usama.arif@linux.dev> wrote:

---

If the stack down by STACK_END_MAGIC has been written you are almost in
'deep do-do land' so task_low_bound() could return an aligned address
and that would let you align down the low address.
(So leaving the very last few words alone.)
It also probably doesn't really matter about the top few words.
So the length could be rounded down.

Alternatively, do STACK_END_MAGIC and STACK_ERASE_POISON even need
to have different values?
(And aren't stacks now allocated with a guard page - so you get a
fault on overflow much earlier than any check for STACK_END_MAGIC.)

But does this code even work in a useful manner.
It seems to stop scanning after finding 128 bytes of KSTACK_ERASE_POISON.
Isn't that going to stop on a function with a large on-stack buffer
(especially since one is likely to be on the deepest part of the stack).
I'd have thought you'd jump down the stack in larger lumps than that
just looking for an unwritten word before even trying to decide if it
was the boundary.

David

Last comment 2026-11-21 Re: [PATCH 2/2] x86/kstack_erase: Use REP STOSQ for large stack fills Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch modifies the x86 kernel's stack erasure function to use REP STOSQ for large stack fills, which is a more efficient instruction than a plain loop. The change was motivated by benchmarking results that showed significant performance improvements (up to 23% reduction in execution time) compared to the original implementation. The patch also includes some discussion about different variants of the erasure function and their relative performance characteristics.

1 reviewer comment — 0 participants
0 participants
David Laight ↳ replying to Usama Arif Inline Review Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer David Laight noted that the patch's performance results are not conclusive, as they only show a significant improvement for large stack fills and do not account for other factors such as branch prediction, cache behavior, and instruction-level parallelism. The reviewer, David Laight, notes that the lfence instruction takes long enough to overlap with setup times for REP STOSQ, making it difficult to measure meaningful timings for short operations. He also mentions that the TSC (Time Stamp Counter) doesn't measure clocks accurately and that CPU speed changes during long-running benchmarks. Additionally, he provides benchmark results using PERF_COUNT_CPU_CYCLES counter and direct execution of rdpmc instructions, showing that REP STOSQ is slower than REP MOVSB for large stack fills.
Show original comment 
On Sat, 21 Nov 2026 13:57:39 +0000
Usama Arif <usama.arif@linux.dev> wrote:

---

The lfence (or mfence - which is the one you really need) take long
enough that they don't give meaningful timings for anything short.
I think they manage to overlap with the setup times for 'rep xxx'.
You also get the problem that the tsc doesn't measure clocks and a lot
of benchmarks run long enough that the cpu speed changes.

I've updated my benchmark code and run some tests on zen5.
This uses the PERF_COUNT_CPU_CYCLES counter but directly executing rdpmc.
I then use data dependencies from the original count to the test code,
and from the test result to the second rdpmc (which nicely has a register
number) to get a time that includes the test function with little overhead.
The clock count for an empty test function is 25, 32 with a memory read.

Each test is run 10 times and the clock count for each shown separately.
The first column includes the cost of the I-cache loads and any branches
that get mispredicted because (in these cases) the length changes.
This is most obvious for the libc calls.

13: len 1024 ok   90   89   90   89   90   89   90   89   90   89 movsb
14: len 2048 ok  112  106  105  106  105  106  105  106  105  106 movsb
15: len   16 ok  108   87   85   65   57   65   57   65   57   65 movsq
16: len  256 ok  119   78   78   78   79   78   79   78   79   78 movsq
17: len  512 ok   81   83   82   83   82   82   82   83   82   82 movsq
18: len 1024 ok   91   91   91   91   91   91   91   91   91   91 movsq
19: len 2048 ok  107  107  107  107  107  107  107  107  107  107 movsq
20: len   16 ok  110   62   56   32   32   32   32   32   32   32 longs
21: len  256 ok  203  128   58   57   57   57   57   47   47   47 longs
22: len  512 ok   97  152  190   95   65   63   65   63   65   63 longs
23: len 1024 ok  226  185  155  100   99   98  100   98   99   98 longs
24: len 2048 ok  201  262  260  179  171  166  170  169  171  166 longs

I added enough barrier() and OPTIMIZER_HIDE_VAR() to get the 'longs'
loop to be sane - it sets/copies two 64bit locations each iteration.
Both tests manage a loop iteration every clock (on zen5).

The cutoff for 'rep stos' is pretty much 1k.
For memcpy() it is a bit lower.

'rep movsq' is slightly slower than 'rep movsb' because of the 'len /= 8'.
But it is much slower for counts of 1 (included above) and 0.

I need to boot up one of my other systems to see how older Intel cpu fair.

David
